Solutions to Problems at Les Houches Summer School on EFT
Marcel Balsiger a, Marios Bounakis b, Mehdi Drissi c,d, John Gargalionis e, Erik Gustafson f, Greg Jackson a, Matthew Leak g, Christopher Lepenik h, Scott Melville i,j, Daniel Moreno k, Michele Tammaro l, Selim Touati m, Timothy Trott n

\maintext

Chapter 0
Solutions

This work details worked solutions to the various problems set by the lecturers
during the course of the Les Houches summer school 2017 on effective field theories in particle physics and cosmology and is based on the final chapter of
[
22
]
.
Further exercises that were added after the school are not solved here, and are left as a challenge for the enterprising reader.

1
Preface

The topic of the CVIII session of
the École de physique des Houches, held in July 2017, was
Effective Field Theory (EFT) in Particle Physics and Cosmology.

In both particle physics and cosmology, our current best understanding
is captured by a “Standard Model” which describes all known interactions,
but which is nevertheless unsatisfactory from a number of points of view.
The hope is that eventually experimental deviations from the Standard
Model are found, which then indicate a road to physics Beyond
the Standard Model (BSM). Ideally this leads, amongst others,
to a verifiable explanation for the origin of Dark Matter.

In order to be sure that a given deviation is really an indication
of BSM physics, Standard Model predictions must be robust and accurate.
This represents a non-trivial challenge, as many
particles, with varying masses and momenta, can participate in
the interactions. Their effects, independently of whether the particles
appear as real or virtual states, need to be systematically accounted for.
As EFTs help disentangling the effects of physics at different scales, they simplify considerably this task.
These scales could be masses (for instance, the light masses of the particles of the Standard Model versus the heavy masses of yet undiscovered particles), momenta (for instance, the hard, collinear and soft momenta playing a role in jets produced in high-energy collisions), or length scales (for instance, the lattice spacing appearing in numerical simulations versus the pion Compton wavelength of interest to low-energy hadronic interactions).

The underlying idea of EFT is that at each scale the relevant physics can be parametrised with appropriate variables, which may change with the scale.
In order to achieve precise predictions there is often no need to know the underlying exact theory: one can work with simpler field theories, describing only the degrees of freedom
relevant at a certain scale, while performing a systematic expansion in
one or more small parameters, generally ratios of well-separated scales.
Hence EFTs are essential tools both for precision analyses within known multi scale theories, such as the Standard Model, and also for a concise parameterisation of hypothetical BSM models.

The goal of this school was
to offer a broad introduction to the foundations and modern applications
of Effective Field Theory in many of its incarnations.
The basic foundations were laid out in two lecture series,
by Matthias Neubert
[
21
]

and Aneesh Manohar
[
20
]
, which review
the field-theoretic background of renormalization, power counting,
and operator classification.
Effective Field Theories for treating systems with
spontaneously broken global symmetries
are introduced by Antonio Pich
[
25
]
,
and applications of EFT to the analysis of inflation
and Large Scale Structure formation were
respectively presented by Cliff Burgess
[
5
]
,
and Tobias Baldauf
[
3
]
.
Ubirajara van Kolck introduced some
uses of Effective Field Theory in nuclear physics
[
26
]
.
In all these lecture series, problems were proposed
to the students, who wrote up the solutions as
this document. There were additional lectures,
without formal problems, by Thomas Mannel about Heavy Quark EFT,
about Soft Collinear Effective Theory by Thomas Becher,
about flavour physics by Luca Silvestrini,
Dark Matter by Junji Hisano and lattice by
Rainer Sommer.

2
Introduction to Renormalisation and the Renormalisation Group (Neubert)

The lectures
[
21
]
provide an overview of renormalization in quantum field theories, with particular attention paid to effective quantum field theories—in which the renormalization of composite operators, operator mixing under scale evolution, and the resummation of large logarithms are important.

This section contains three introductory problems, which cover propagators and 1PI diagrams, superficial degrees of divergence and Renormalization Group Equations.

Exercise 2.1
.

Express the propagator of a spin-1 field in terms of 1PI self-energy diagrams.

SOLUTION:

From the Lagrangian, we can derive the classical equations of motion,

\mathcal{L}=-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}^{2}+\frac{1}{\xi}(\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu})^{2}+J_{\mu}A^{\mu}\;\;\implies\;\;\left[p^{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}-\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi}\right)p_{\mu}p_{\nu}\right]A_{\nu}=J_{\nu}

(1)

Inverting this provides the Feynman propagator (classical Green’s function),

P_{\mu\nu}=\frac{-i}{p^{2}}\left[\eta_{\mu\nu}-(1-\xi)\frac{p_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right]

(2)

where the
+i\epsilon
Feynman prescription for the poles is implicit.

The 1PI diagrams must satisfy the Ward identity by gauge invariance, and so we can write them as,

p^{2}\Pi(p)X^{\mu\nu}\;\;\;\;\text{where}\;\;\;\;X^{\mu\nu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}-p^{\mu}p^{\nu}/p^{2}

(3)

where
X^{\mu\nu}
is a projection onto the physical (transverse) polarization states. As a projection, it is idempotent,

X^{\mu\nu}\eta_{\nu\alpha}X^{\alpha\beta}=X^{\mu\beta}

and it also annihilates the gauge-fixing term as
X^{\mu\nu}p_{\mu}=0
. It is then straightforward to resum a geometric series of 1PI blobs,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=P^{\mu\nu}+P^{\mu\alpha}X_{\alpha\beta}P^{\beta\nu}+PXPXP+....

(4)

\displaystyle=\frac{-i\xi p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{4}}-\frac{i}{p^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\Pi(p^{2})\right)^{n}X^{\mu\nu}

(5)

\displaystyle=\frac{-i\xi p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{4}}-\frac{i}{p^{2}(1-\Pi(p^{2}))}\left[\eta^{\mu\nu}-\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right]

(6)

Having extracted the factor of
k^{2}X^{\mu\nu}
from the 1PI self-energy diagrams, we find that the remaining
\Pi(k^{2})
function shifts the residue of the pole at
k^{2}=0
in the resummed propagator to
1/(1-\Pi(0))
. This is exactly analogous to the residue renormalization for scalars.

{exercisenb}

Find the superficial degree of divergence for 1PI QCD Feynman graphs.

SOLUTION:

Counting powers of loop momenta, we define,

D=4L-P_{q}-2P_{g}-P_{c}+V_{3g}

(7)

as the Grassmann-valued quarks and ghosts have propagators with only one power of momenta, while the bosonic gluons has two powers of momenta. The three gluon vertex must also contain one power of momenta (by Lorentz invariance).

From the Euler characteristic,

L=P_{q}+P_{g}+P_{c}-(V_{qg}+V_{3g}+V_{4g}+V_{cg})+1

and from counting the total numbers of quark, ghost and gluon legs,

\displaystyle 2P_{q}+N_{q}

\displaystyle=2V_{qg}

(8)

\displaystyle 2P_{c}+N_{c}

\displaystyle=V_{cg}

(9)

\displaystyle 2P_{g}+N_{g}

\displaystyle=V_{qg}+V_{cg}+3V_{3g}+4V_{4g}

(10)

Putting these together, one finds that,

D=4-N_{g}-\frac{3}{2}(N_{q}+N_{c})

(11)

where
N_{X}
are the numbers of external particles. Although all physical diagrams have
N_{c}=0
, it is useful to keep it explicitly for analyzing subdiagrams.

{exercisenb}

From the RGEs,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha_{s}}\beta

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon-Z^{-1}_{\alpha}\frac{d}{d\text{ln}\mu}Z_{\alpha}

(12)

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{Z_{m}^{\prime}}\frac{d}{d\text{ln}\mu}Z_{m}^{\prime}

(13)

in the MS scheme,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha_{s}}\beta

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon-\beta Z^{-1}_{\alpha}\frac{d}{d\alpha_{s}}Z_{\alpha}

(14)

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta}{Z_{m}^{\prime}}\frac{d}{d\alpha_{s}}Z_{m}^{\prime}

(15)

derive expressions for
\beta(\alpha_{s},\epsilon),Z_{\alpha}(\alpha_{s},\epsilon),\gamma(\alpha_{s},\epsilon),Z_{\alpha}(\alpha_{s},\epsilon)
as perturbative series in
\epsilon
.

SOLUTION:

Assume that
\beta
is a smooth function of
\epsilon
,

\beta=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\epsilon^{k}\beta^{(k)}

(16)

The renormalization
Z_{\alpha}
receives pole contributions
1/\epsilon^{k}
from
k
-loop diagrams, i.e.

Z=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\epsilon^{-k}Z_{\alpha}^{(k)}

(17)

where
Z_{\alpha}^{(k)}\sim\alpha_{s}^{k}
.
The RGEs can be written,

\beta(\alpha,\epsilon)=\frac{-2\epsilon\alpha_{s}Z_{\alpha}}{\left(1+\alpha_{s}\frac{d}{d\alpha_{s}}\right)Z_{\alpha}}

(18)

and hold for all
\epsilon
. We can therefore equate the coefficients of each power of
\epsilon
,

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\epsilon^{k}\beta^{(k)}=-2\epsilon\alpha_{s}+2\alpha_{s}^{2}\frac{d}{d\alpha_{s}}Z_{\alpha}^{(1)}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)

(19)

and conclude that,

\beta(\alpha_{s},\epsilon)=2\alpha_{s}^{2}\frac{d}{d\alpha_{s}}Z_{\alpha}^{(1)}(\alpha_{s})-2\alpha_{s}\epsilon.

(20)

Substituting this into the RGE, we find an infinite number of consistency conditions which fix all
Z_{\alpha}^{(k>1)}
in terms of
Z_{\alpha}^{(1)}(\alpha_{s})
,

\displaystyle\frac{dZ_{\alpha}^{(k+1)}}{d\alpha_{s}}=\frac{dZ_{\alpha}^{(1)}}{d\alpha_{s}}\left[1+\alpha_{s}\frac{d}{d\alpha_{s}}\right]Z_{\alpha}^{(k)}\;\;\;\;\forall\;\;\;\;k\geq 1

(21)

Note that by taking repeated
\alpha_{s}
derivatives, this gives every
\alpha_{s}^{n}
coefficient of
Z_{\alpha}^{(k+1)}
(we don’t have
Z_{\alpha_{s}}^{(k+1)}|_{\alpha_{s}=0}
, but we know that this is zero).

Similarly, assume that
\gamma_{m}
is a smooth function of
\epsilon
,

\gamma_{m}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\epsilon^{k}\gamma_{m}^{(k)}

(22)

and then the positive powers of
\epsilon
in the RGE give,

\gamma_{m}=2\alpha_{s}\frac{d}{d\alpha_{s}}Z_{m}^{\prime(1)}(\alpha_{s})

(23)

and similarly we have consistency relations,

\frac{dZ_{m}^{\prime(k+1)}}{d\alpha_{s}}=\left[\frac{dZ_{m}^{\prime(1)}}{d\alpha_{s}}+\alpha_{s}\frac{dZ_{\alpha}^{(1)}}{d\alpha_{s}}\frac{d}{d\alpha_{s}}\right]Z_{m}^{\prime(k)}

(24)

Physically, this is because the higher order poles in
Z
come from multiple subdivergences, rather than than a genuine
k
loop divergence.

3
Introduction to Effective Field Theories (Manohar)

The lectures
[
20
]
covered introductory material on EFTs as used in
high-energy physics to compute experimentally observable quantities.
The following exercise solutions treat power counting, loop
corrections, field redefinitions and their relation to the equations
of motion, decoupling of heavy particles, naive dimensional analysis,
and the Standard Model Effective Field Theory (SMEFT) and many more
basic concepts.

Useful references for solving the problems are

EFT:
[

,

]

•

[

,

]

•

Matching in HQET and field redefinitions:
[
18
]

•

Invariants:
[
12
,
9
,
15
,

,

]

•

SMEFT:
[
13
,

,
2
,
1
]

Equations from the lecture notes are referred to with a prefix L, e.g. eqn (L1.1).

Exercise 3.1
.

Show that for a
connected
graph,
V-I+L=1
, where
V
is the number of vertices,
I
is the number of internal lines, and
L
is the number of loops. What is the formula if the graph has
n
connected components?

SOLUTION:

Consider a connected graph
G
where
V
is the number of vertices,
I
is the number of internal lines, and
L
is the number of loops. Since the identity does not depend on the external lines, erase them. Take an internal line (edge) that can be deleted without making the graph disconnected, and remove it. Then clearly
I\to I-1
. If the edge joins vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
and removing the edge leaves the graph connected, there must be a second path through the graph between
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, i.e. there is a loop which is removed when the edge is removed, so
L\to L-1
. The operation leaves
V-I+L
invariant. Proceed this way till removing an edge makes the graph disconnected, i.e. there are no loops, and we are left with a tree graph.

Now pick any vertex
v
in the graph, and move through the graph without retracing your path. There are no loops, so the path does not end back at
v
. Since the number of vertices is finite, eventually the path must end at a vertex (a “leaf node” of the tree graph). Remove this last node and edge. Then
V\to V-1
and
I\to I-1
keeping
V-I+L
. Keep repeating this process until
I=0
. Then we have a graph with one vertex
V=1
,
I=0
,
L=0
,
V-I+L=1
. Since our operators preserved
V-I+L=1
, this completes the proof. If there are
n
connected components, the formula holds for each component, so the total is

V-I+L=n
since the components have no vertices, edges or loops in common.

{exercisenb}

Work out the transformation of fermion bilinears
\overline{\psi}(\mathbf{x},t)\,\Gamma\,\chi(\mathbf{x},t)
under
C
,
P
,
T
, where
\Gamma=P_{L},P_{R},\gamma^{\mu}P_{L},\gamma^{\mu}P_{R},\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L},\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{R}
. Use your results to find the transformations under
CP
,
CT
,
PT
and
CPT
.

SOLUTION:

Under parity
\mathscr{P}
, charge conjugation
\mathscr{C}
and time-reversal
\mathscr{T}
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}\psi(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{P}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{0}\psi(-\mathbf{x},t),

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}\psi(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{C}^{-1}

\displaystyle=i\gamma^{2}\psi(\mathbf{x},t)^{\dagger T},

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}\psi(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{T}^{-1}

\displaystyle=i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{3}\psi(\mathbf{x},-t)\,.

(1)

Note that
\mathscr{C}
is a
unitary
operator, and
\mathscr{T}
is
antiunitary
despite the
\dagger
for the
\mathscr{C}
transformation and not for the
\mathscr{T}
transformation.
\mathscr{P},\mathscr{C}
are operators and commute with
\gamma
matrices;
\mathscr{T}
complex conjugates the
\gamma
matrices. Transformations of
\overline{\psi}
are given by taking conjugates of the above.

Under parity,

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}\overline{\psi}(\mathbf{x},t)\Gamma\chi(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{P}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{P}\overline{\psi}(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{P}^{-1}\Gamma\mathscr{P}\chi(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{P}^{-1}=\overline{\psi}(-\mathbf{x},t)\gamma^{0}\Gamma\gamma^{0}\chi(-\mathbf{x},t).

(2)

Under charge conjugation,

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}\overline{\psi}(\mathbf{x},t)\Gamma\chi(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{C}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{C}\overline{\psi}(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{C}^{-1}\Gamma\mathscr{C}\chi(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{C}^{-1}=\psi(\mathbf{x},t)^{T}i\gamma^{2}\gamma^{0}\Gamma i\gamma^{2}\chi(\mathbf{x},t)^{\dagger T}.

(3)

Taking the transpose (and including the Fermi minus sign for the exchange of operators),

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}\overline{\psi}(\mathbf{x},t)\Gamma\chi(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{C}^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\chi(\mathbf{x},t)^{\dagger}(i\gamma^{2})^{T}\Gamma^{T}(\gamma^{0})^{T}(i\gamma^{2})^{T}\psi(\mathbf{x},t)

\displaystyle=-\overline{\chi}(\mathbf{x},t)(i\gamma^{0}\gamma^{2})\Gamma^{T}(i\gamma^{0}\gamma^{2})\psi(\mathbf{x},t)

\displaystyle=\overline{\chi}(\mathbf{x},t)\,C\,\Gamma^{T}C^{-1}\psi(\mathbf{x},t),

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=i\gamma^{0}\gamma^{2}

(4)

Under time-reversal,

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}\overline{\psi}(\mathbf{x},t)\Gamma\chi(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{T}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{T}\overline{\psi}(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{T}^{-1}\mathscr{T}\Gamma\mathscr{T}^{-1}\mathscr{T}\chi(\mathbf{x},t)\mathscr{T}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\psi}(\mathbf{x},-t)\gamma^{0}i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{3}\gamma^{0}\Gamma^{*}i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{3}\chi(\mathbf{x},-t)

\displaystyle=\overline{\psi}(\mathbf{x},-t)(i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{3})\Gamma^{*}(i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{3})\chi(\mathbf{x},-t)

\displaystyle=\overline{\psi}(\mathbf{x},-t)\,T\,\Gamma^{*}T^{-1}\chi(\mathbf{x},-t),

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{3}

(5)

The transformations can then be determined by computing
\gamma^{0}\Gamma\gamma^{0}
,
(i\gamma^{0}\gamma^{2})\Gamma^{T}(i\gamma^{0}\gamma^{2})
and
(i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{3})\Gamma^{*}(i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{3})
to give the results in the table. The second line gives the coordinate arguments, and

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mu&\mbox{if $\mu=0$}\\
-\mu&\mbox{if $\mu=1,2,3$}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{|c|ccc|}\hline\cr&\mathscr{C}&\mathscr{P}&\mathscr{T}\\
\hline\cr(\mathbf{x},t)&(\mathbf{x},t)&(-\mathbf{x},t)&(\mathbf{x},-t)\\
\hline\cr\bar{\chi}P_{L}\psi&\bar{\psi}P_{L}\chi&\bar{\chi}P_{R}\psi&\bar{\chi}P_{L}\psi\\
\bar{\chi}P_{R}\psi&\bar{\psi}P_{R}\chi&\bar{\chi}P_{L}\psi&\bar{\chi}P_{R}\psi\\
\bar{\chi}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\psi&-\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}P_{R}\chi&\bar{\chi}\gamma^{\hat{\mu}}P_{R}\psi&\bar{\chi}\gamma^{\hat{\mu}}P_{L}\psi\\
\bar{\chi}\gamma^{\mu}P_{R}\psi&-\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\chi&\bar{\chi}\gamma^{\hat{\mu}}P_{L}\psi&\bar{\chi}\gamma^{\hat{\mu}}P_{R}\psi\\
\bar{\chi}\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\psi&-\bar{\psi}\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\chi&\bar{\chi}\sigma^{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}P_{R}\psi&-\bar{\chi}\sigma^{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}P_{L}\psi\\
\bar{\chi}\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{R}\psi&-\bar{\psi}\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{R}\chi&\bar{\chi}\sigma^{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}P_{L}\psi&-\bar{\chi}\sigma^{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}P_{R}\psi\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Combining the above gives

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{|c|cccc|}\hline\cr&\mathscr{CP}&\mathscr{PT}&\mathscr{CT}&\mathscr{CPT}\\
\hline\cr(\mathbf{x},t)&(-\mathbf{x},t)&(-\mathbf{x},-t)&(\mathbf{x},-t)&(-\mathbf{x},-t)\\
\hline\cr\bar{\chi}P_{L}\psi&\bar{\psi}P_{R}\chi&\bar{\chi}P_{R}\psi&\bar{\psi}P_{L}\chi&\bar{\psi}P_{R}\chi\\
\bar{\chi}P_{R}\psi&\bar{\psi}P_{L}\chi&\bar{\chi}P_{L}\psi&\bar{\psi}P_{R}\chi&\bar{\psi}P_{L}\chi\\
\bar{\chi}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\psi&-\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\hat{\mu}}P_{L}\chi&\bar{\chi}\gamma^{\mu}P_{R}\psi&-\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\hat{\mu}}P_{R}\chi&-\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\chi\\
\bar{\chi}\gamma^{\mu}P_{R}\psi&-\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\hat{\mu}}P_{R}\chi&\bar{\chi}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\psi&-\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\hat{\mu}}P_{L}\chi&-\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}P_{R}\chi\\
\bar{\chi}\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\psi&-\bar{\psi}\sigma^{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}P_{R}\chi&-\bar{\chi}\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{R}\psi&\bar{\psi}\sigma^{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}P_{L}\chi&\bar{\psi}\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{R}\chi\\
\bar{\chi}\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{R}\psi&-\bar{\psi}\sigma^{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}P_{L}\chi&-\bar{\chi}\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\psi&\bar{\psi}\sigma^{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}P_{R}\chi&\bar{\psi}\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\chi\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Note that for any operator
O(\mathbf{x},t)
,
\mathscr{CPT}
transforms it to
(-1)^{n}O^{\dagger}(-\mathbf{x},-t)
where
n
is the number of Lorentz indices.
Thus the Lagrange density transforms as
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},t)\to\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}(-\mathbf{x},-t)
, and a Hermitian action is
\mathscr{CPT}
invariant.

{exercisenb}

Show that for
SU(N)
,

\displaystyle[T^{A}]^{\alpha}_{\ \beta}\,[T^{A}]^{\lambda}_{\ \sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\delta^{\alpha}_{\sigma}\,\delta^{\lambda}_{\beta}-\frac{1}{2N}\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\,\delta^{\lambda}_{\sigma},

(6)

where the
SU(N)
generators are normalized to
\text{Tr}\,T^{A}T^{B}=\delta^{AB}/2
. From this, show that

\displaystyle\ \delta^{\alpha}_{\ \beta}\,\delta^{\lambda}_{\ \sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\delta^{\alpha}_{\sigma}\,\delta^{\lambda}_{\beta}+2[T^{A}]^{\alpha}_{\ \sigma}\,[T^{A}]^{\lambda}_{\ \beta},

\displaystyle[T^{A}]^{\alpha}_{\ \beta}\,[T^{A}]^{\lambda}_{\ \sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{N^{2}-1}{2N^{2}}\delta^{\alpha}_{\sigma}\,\delta^{\lambda}_{\beta}-\frac{1}{N}[T^{A}]^{\alpha}_{\ \sigma}\,[T^{A}]^{\lambda}_{\ \beta}.

(7)

SOLUTION:

The identity and the
T^{a}
(
a=1,...,N^{2}-1
) provide a basis for
N\times N
matrices, so any such matrix can be decomposed as

A=c_{0}\mathbbm{1}+c_{a}T^{a}\,.

(8)

If
A
is Hermitian, the coefficients are real.
Now, assume that the Killing form is normalized as,

\text{Tr}\left(T^{a}T^{b}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\delta^{ab}\,

(9)

and therefore

\text{Tr}\,A=Nc_{0},\;\;\;\;\text{Tr}\,T^{a}A=\frac{1}{2}c_{a}\,.

(10)

With explicit indices, this tells us that,

A_{ij}=\left(\frac{A_{k\ell}\delta_{k\ell}}{N}\right)\delta_{ij}+\left(2T^{a}_{k\ell}A_{\ell k}\right)T^{a}_{ij}\,,

(11)

\implies\;\;A_{\ell k}\left[\delta_{i\ell}\delta_{jk}-\frac{1}{N}\delta_{k\ell}\delta_{ij}-2T^{a}_{ij}T^{a}_{k\ell}\right]=0\,,

on picking out the coefficient of
A_{\ell k}
.
As this identity holds for all elements
A_{ij}
of all Hermitian matrices, we conclude that the square bracket vanishes identically, and thus arrive at the desired Fierz identity,

T^{a}_{ij}T^{a}_{k\ell}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{i\ell}\delta_{jk}-\frac{1}{2N}\delta_{ij}\delta_{k\ell}\,,

(12)

which is eqn (L1.2).
Rewriting this equation as

\frac{1}{2N}\delta_{ij}\delta_{k\ell}+T^{a}_{ij}T^{a}_{k\ell}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{i\ell}\delta_{jk}\,,

(13)

multiplying by
2
, and renaming the indices gives the first of eqn (1.3). Adding eqn (1.1) and the corresponding equation with
\beta\leftrightarrow\sigma
multiplied by
1/N
gives the second of eqn (1.3).

{exercisenb}

Spinor Fierz identities are relations of the form

\displaystyle(\overline{A}\,\Gamma_{1}\,B)(\overline{C}\,\Gamma_{2}\,D)=\sum_{ij}c_{ij}(\overline{C}\,\Gamma_{i}\,B)(\overline{A}\,\Gamma_{j}\,D)

where
A,B,C,D
are fermion fields, and
c_{ij}
are numbers. They are much simpler if written in terms of chiral fields using
\Gamma_{i}=P_{L},P_{R},\gamma^{\mu}P_{L},\gamma^{\mu}P_{R},\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L},\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{R}
, rather than Dirac fields. Work out the Fierz relations for

\displaystyle(\overline{A}P_{L}B)(\overline{C}P_{L}D),

\displaystyle(\overline{A}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}B)(\overline{C}\gamma_{\mu}P_{L}D),

\displaystyle(\overline{A}\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}B)(\overline{C}\sigma_{\mu\nu}P_{L}D),

\displaystyle(\overline{A}P_{L}B)(\overline{C}P_{R}D),

\displaystyle(\overline{A}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}B)(\overline{C}\gamma_{\mu}P_{R}D),

\displaystyle(\overline{A}\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}B)(\overline{C}\sigma_{\mu\nu}P_{R}D).

Do not forget the Fermi minus sign. The
P_{R}\otimes P_{R}
identities are obtained from the
P_{L}\otimes P_{L}
identities by using
L\leftrightarrow R
.

SOLUTION:

Define

\gamma^{5}=\gamma_{5}=i\gamma^{0}\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{3},\;\;\;\;\sigma^{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{2}[\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}]

(14)

and raise/lower spacetime indices with
\eta_{\mu\nu}=(+1,-1,-1,-1)
.

Use a chiral basis,

\left\{\Gamma^{A}\right\}=\left\{P_{R},P_{L},\gamma^{\mu}P_{R},\gamma^{\mu}P_{L},\sigma^{\mu\nu}\right\}\,.

\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{5}
are four elements of the basis for
\mu=\{0,1,2,3\}
, and
\sigma^{\mu\nu}
are six elements of the basis for
\mu\nu=\{01,02,03,12,23,31\}
. The 16 elements of
\Gamma^{A}
are linearly independent and any
4\times 4
matrix can be written as a linear combination of
\Gamma^{A}
.
Define a dual basis,

\left\{\Gamma_{A}\right\}=\left\{P_{R},P_{L},\gamma_{\mu}P_{L},\gamma_{\mu}P_{R},\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{\mu\nu}\right\}

such that,

\text{Tr}[\Gamma_{A}\Gamma^{B}]=2\delta^{B}_{A}.

Any
4\times 4
matrix can be written as

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=c_{A}\Gamma^{A}

\displaystyle c_{A}=\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\left[X\Gamma_{A}\right]

(15)

Then it is straightforward to project the bilinears onto this basis,

\left(\Gamma^{A}\right)\left[\Gamma^{B}\right]=-\frac{1}{4}\text{Tr}\left[\Gamma^{A}\Gamma_{C}\Gamma^{B}\Gamma_{D}\right]\;\;\left(\Gamma^{D}\right]\left[\Gamma^{C}\right)

(16)

where the overall minus sign accounts for the anti-commutativity of the fermion fields, and the type of parenthesis indicates whether the
\Gamma
matrix is contracted with
\overline{A}
,
B
,
\overline{C}
or
D
.

Evaluating eqn (
16
) for the required cases:

\displaystyle\left(P_{L}\right)\left[P_{L}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\text{Tr}\left[P_{L}\Gamma_{C}P_{L}\Gamma_{D}\right]\left(\Gamma^{D}\right]\left[\Gamma^{C}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(P_{L}\right]\left[P_{L}\right)-\frac{1}{16}\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\right]\left(\sigma_{\mu\nu}\right]-\frac{i}{16}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\right]\left(\sigma_{\alpha\beta}\right]

(17)

[Note that
\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\right]\left(\sigma_{\mu\nu}\right]
sums over both
\mu<\nu
and
\mu>\nu
and so is twice the sum over
\sigma^{\mu\nu}
in the basis set
\Gamma^{A}
.]

\displaystyle\left(P_{L}\right)\left[P_{R}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\gamma^{\mu}P_{R}\right]\left(\gamma_{\mu}P_{L}\right]

(18)

\displaystyle\left(\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\right)\left[\gamma_{\mu}P_{L}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\right]\left(\gamma_{\mu}P_{L}\right]

(19)

\displaystyle\left(\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\right)\left[\gamma_{\mu}P_{R}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\text{Tr}\left[P_{R}\gamma^{\mu}\Gamma_{C}P_{L}\gamma_{\mu}\Gamma_{D}\right]\;\left(\Gamma^{D}\right]\left[\Gamma^{C}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\text{Tr}\left[P_{R}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}^{2}\gamma_{\mu}P_{R}\right]\;\left(R\right]\left[L\right)

\displaystyle=-2\left(R\right]\left[L\right)\;\;\;\;\text{as}\;\;\;\;\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{\mu}=4\mathbbm{1},\;\;\;\;\text{Tr}\left[P_{R}^{4}\right]=\text{Tr}\left[P_{R}\right]=2

(20)

\displaystyle\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right)\left[\sigma_{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right]

\displaystyle=-6\left(P_{L}\right]\left(P_{L}\right]-\frac{1}{4}\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\right]\left(\sigma_{\mu\nu}\right]-\frac{i}{4}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\left(\sigma_{\mu\nu}\right]\left(\sigma_{\alpha\beta}\right]

(21)

\displaystyle\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right)\left[\sigma_{\mu\nu}P_{R}\right]

\displaystyle=0

(22)

Note that

\frac{i}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\sigma_{\alpha\beta}=-\gamma^{5}\sigma^{\mu\nu}

(
\epsilon_{0123}=+1
)
which can be used to replace the
\epsilon
tensors by
\gamma_{5}
. eqn (
3
) becomes

\displaystyle\left(P_{L}\right)\left[P_{L}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(P_{L}\right]\left[P_{L}\right)-\frac{1}{16}\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\right]\left(\sigma_{\mu\nu}\right]+\frac{1}{16}\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\right]\left(\gamma_{5}\sigma_{\mu\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(P_{L}\right]\left[P_{L}\right)-\frac{1}{8}\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right]\left(\sigma_{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right]

(23)

In the last line, we have put back the projectors on
\sigma^{\mu\nu}
, since they are contracted with left-handed fields, and used
\gamma_{5}P_{L}=-P_{L}
. Similarly, eqn (
21
) becomes

\displaystyle\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right)\left[\sigma_{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right]

\displaystyle=-6\left(P_{L}\right]\left(P_{L}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right]\left(\sigma_{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right]

(24)

To summarize, the identities (including the Fermi minus sign) are:

\displaystyle\left(P_{L}\right)\left[P_{L}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(P_{L}\right]\left[P_{L}\right)-\frac{1}{8}\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right]\left(\sigma_{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right]

\displaystyle\left(P_{L}\right)\left[P_{R}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\gamma^{\mu}P_{R}\right]\left(\gamma_{\mu}P_{L}\right]

\displaystyle\left(\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\right)\left[\gamma_{\mu}P_{L}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\right]\left(\gamma_{\mu}P_{L}\right]

\displaystyle\left(\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\right)\left[\gamma_{\mu}P_{R}\right]

\displaystyle=-2\left(R\right]\left[L\right)

\displaystyle\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right)\left[\sigma_{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right]

\displaystyle=-6\left(P_{L}\right]\left(P_{L}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right]\left(\sigma_{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right]

\displaystyle\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}P_{L}\right)\left[\sigma_{\mu\nu}P_{R}\right]

\displaystyle=0

(25)

{exercisenb}

Compute the mass renormalization factor
Z_{m}
in QCD at one loop. Use this to determine the one-loop mass anomalous dimension
\gamma_{m}
,

\displaystyle\mu\frac{\text{d}m}{\text{d}\mu}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{m}m,

(26)

by differentiating
m_{0}=Z_{m}m
, and noting that
m_{0}
is
\mu
-independent.

SOLUTION:

The Lagrangian for massless QCD is

\mathcal{L}_{m=0}=\bar{q}_{0}\not{D}q_{0}-\frac{1}{4}G_{\mu\nu,0}^{A}G^{\mu\nu}_{A,0}\,,

(27)

where
q_{0}
is the bare quark field,
G_{\mu\nu,0}
is the bare gluon field strength tensor and
\not{D}
is the covariant derivative (we are only interested in quark-gluon interactions, so other gauge bosons are neglected here)

D_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}-ig_{0,s}t_{A}G^{A}_{\mu,0}\,,

(28)

where
t_{A}
are the
SU(3)
generators and
g_{0,s}
the respective bare gauge coupling;
G^{A}_{\mu,0}
is the bare gluon field. The insertion of the quark mass operator
\mathcal{O}_{f}=m_{0}\bar{q}_{0}q_{0}
, where
m_{0}
is the bare quark mass, modifies the Lagrangian

\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{m=0}-\mathcal{O}_{f}\,.

(29)

Figure 1:
One loop correction to the mass operator.

To renormalize the theory we define the renormalization constants

q_{0}=Z_{q}^{1/2}q\,,\quad m_{0}=Z_{m}m\,,

(30)

where the fields without subscripts are renormalized fields. These constants will absorb the divergences. The other renormalization constants (for the gauge field and coupling) are not needed for this problem. In the
{\overline{\rm MS}}
scheme we impose that only the
1/\epsilon
pole and the dim-reg constants are absorbed, thus giving

Z_{i}=1+z_{i,1}\frac{g^{2}_{s}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\hat{\epsilon}}+\dots\,,

(31)

where the
z_{i,1}
are
\mu
-independent constants (all the
\mu
dependence is in the coupling constant).

To solve this problem we are interested in the Lagrangian and operator counterterms

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{ct}}

\displaystyle=\left[(Z_{q}-1)\not{p}\right]\,,

\displaystyle O_{f,\text{ct}}

\displaystyle=(Z_{q}Z_{m}-1)m\bar{q}q

(32)

which make the one-loop corrections finite.

The loop integral with the insertion of the operator
\mathcal{O}_{f}
is showed in Fig.
1

I_{\bar{q}q}=m(ig_{s})^{2}\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\gamma_{\alpha}t_{A}\frac{i\not{k}}{k^{2}}\frac{i\not{k}}{k^{2}}\gamma_{\beta}t_{B}\left(\frac{-ig^{\alpha\beta}\delta^{AB}}{(k-p)^{2}}\right)\,.

(33)

Notice that we are only keeping the large
k
region, since we are only interested in the divergent part. We can simplify this expression noting that
\delta^{AB}t_{A}t_{B}=C_{F}=4/3
is the eigenvalue of the Casimir operator and that, in
d
dimensions,
g^{\alpha\beta}\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\beta}=d
. Also
\not{k}\not{k}=k^{2}
, thus we have

I_{\bar{q}q}=-img_{s}^{2}C_{F}\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{d}{k^{2}(k-p)^{2}}\,.

(34)

Introducing the Feynman parameters we have

I_{\bar{q}q}=-ig_{s}^{2}mC_{F}\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}dx\frac{d}{\left[(1-x)k^{2}+x(k-p)^{2}\right]^{2}}\,.

(35)

Define
\ell=k-xp
and
\Delta=-x(1-x)p^{2}
, so we can write

I_{\bar{q}q}=-ig_{s}^{2}mC_{F}\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}dx\frac{d}{\left[\ell-\Delta\right]^{2}}=g_{s}^{2}C_{F}\int_{0}^{1}dx\frac{d}{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\frac{\Gamma(2-\frac{d}{2})}{\Gamma(2)}\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{\Delta}\right)^{2-\frac{d}{2}}\,.

(36)

Expanding and integrating, we get

I_{\bar{q}q}=\frac{g_{s}^{2}mC_{F}}{(4\pi)^{2}}4\left(\frac{1}{\hat{\epsilon}}-{\rm ln}\frac{-p^{2}}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{3}{2}\right)\,,

(37)

so that the divergent part is

I_{\bar{q}q,\text{div}}=\frac{4mg_{s}^{2}C_{F}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\hat{\epsilon}}\,.

(38)

The counterterm
Z_{q}Z_{m}m
cancels the divergence,

Z_{q}Z_{m}=1-\frac{4g_{s}^{2}C_{F}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\hat{\epsilon}}\,.

(39)

Figure 2:
One-loop diagram for the quark self energy.

We need now to calculate the one-loop contribution to the quark propagator, shown in Fig.
2
. This gives

\begin{split}I_{2}&=(ig_{s})^{2}\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\gamma_{\alpha}t_{A}\frac{i\left(\not{p}-\not{k}\right)}{\left(p-k\right)^{2}}\gamma_{\beta}t_{B}\left(\frac{-ig^{\alpha\beta}\delta^{AB}}{k^{2}}\right)\\
&=-g_{s}^{2}C_{F}\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{\gamma_{\alpha}\left(\not{p}-\not{k}\right)\gamma^{\alpha}}{\left(p-k\right)^{2}k^{2}}\,,\end{split}

(40)

where we used again the Casimir operator eigenvalue. To simplify the Dirac structure in the numerator, we use the
d
-dimensional identity

\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\mu}=(2-d)\gamma^{\nu}\,,

(41)

thus

I_{2}=-g_{s}^{2}C_{F}\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{(2-d)\left(\not{p}-\not{k}\right)}{\left(p-k\right)^{2}k^{2}}\,.

(42)

We introduce now the Feynman parameter and get

\begin{split}I_{2}&=-g_{s}^{2}C_{F}\mu^{2\epsilon}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{(2-d)\left(\not{p}-\not{k}\right)}{\left[x\left(p-k\right)^{2}+(1-x)k^{2}\right]^{2}}\\
&=-g_{s}^{2}C_{F}\mu^{2\epsilon}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int\frac{d^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{(2-d)\left((1-x)\not{p}-\not{\ell}\right)}{\left[\ell^{2}-\Delta\right]^{2}}\,,\end{split}

(43)

where we defined
\ell=k-xp
and
\Delta=-(1-x)xp^{2}
. The term linear in
\ell
vanishes, so the only contribution is

\begin{split}I_{2}&=-g_{s}^{2}C_{F}\not{p}\int_{0}^{1}dx(1-x)\frac{(2-d)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\frac{\Gamma\left(2-\frac{d}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(2)}\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{\Delta}\right)^{2-\frac{d}{2}}\\
&=g_{s}^{2}C_{F}\not{p}\int_{0}^{1}dx(1-x)\frac{2(1+\epsilon)}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\hat{\epsilon}}-{\rm ln}\left(\frac{-(x(1-x)p^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right]\,,\end{split}

(44)

where we used
d=4-2\epsilon
, from which we have
2-d=-2(1+\epsilon)
. The divergent part is then

I_{2,\text{div}}=\frac{g_{s}^{2}C_{F}\not{p}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\hat{\epsilon}}\,.

(45)

The counterterm
Z_{q}
cancels the divergence,

Z_{q}=1-\frac{g_{s}^{2}C_{F}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\hat{\epsilon}}\,.

(46)

We can now find the anomalous dimension. This can be computed from the equation

\gamma_{\mathcal{O}}=\frac{1}{Z_{m}}\frac{d\,Z_{m}}{d\ln\mu}=-2g_{s}^{2}\frac{dz_{m,1}}{dg_{s}^{2}}\,,

(47)

where the last step can be deduced from eqn (
31
) (see Neubert’s lectures). This means that
\gamma_{\mathcal{O}}
can be directly obtained from the
1/\hat{\epsilon}
pole constant. From eqns (
46
) and (
39
),

Z_{m}=\frac{1-4\frac{g_{s}^{2}C_{F}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\hat{\epsilon}}}{1-\frac{g_{s}^{2}C_{F}p}{(4\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\hat{\epsilon}}}=1-3\frac{g_{s}^{2}C_{F}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\hat{\epsilon}}+{\mathcal{O}}(g_{s}^{4})\,.

(48)

Finally, we can now plug this result into (
47
) and get

\gamma_{\mathcal{O}}=6\frac{g_{s}^{2}C_{F}}{(4\pi)^{2}}=C_{F}\frac{3\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}=\frac{2\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\,.

(49)

The anomalous dimension of
m
is the negative of that for
O
,

\gamma_{m}=-C_{F}\frac{3\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}=-\frac{2\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\,.

(50)

The individual graphs, and thus
Z_{q}
and
Z_{m}Z_{q}
depend on the choice of gauge, but
Z_{m}
and
\gamma_{m}
are gauge independent.

{exercisenb}

Verify eqn (L3.16) and eqn (L3.17).

SOLUTION:

The one-loop QCD running,

\frac{1}{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{s}(Q)}+\frac{33-2n_{f}}{6\pi}\text{log}\left(\frac{\mu}{Q}\right)

(51)

where
n_{f}
is the number of active flavors between
\mu
and
Q
, allows us to relate a coupling fixed at 1 TeV to the hadronization scale
\Lambda_{\rm QCD}
(at which
1/\alpha_{s}(\Lambda_{\rm QCD})=0
) by running down through the heavy quark thresholds,

\frac{6\pi}{\alpha_{s}(1\text{TeV})}=21\,\text{log}\frac{1\text{TeV}}{m_{t}}+23\,\text{log}\frac{m_{t}}{m_{b}}+25\,\text{log}\frac{m_{b}}{m_{c}}+27\,\text{log}\frac{m_{c}}{\Lambda_{\rm QCD}}

(52)

\implies\;\;\;\;\Lambda_{\rm QCD}=e^{-\frac{6\pi}{27\alpha_{s}(1\text{TeV})}}(1\,\text{TeV})^{21/27}\left(m_{t}\,m_{b}\,m_{c}\right)^{2/27}

The proton mass is proportional to
\Lambda_{\rm QCD}
, and so scales as
m_{t}^{2/27}
when the coupling is fixed at an energy
\mu>m_{t}
(providing other quark masses are held fixed). Applying
m_{t}\text{d}/\text{d}m_{t}
to

\frac{6\pi}{\alpha_{s}(1\text{TeV})}=21\,\text{log}\frac{1\text{TeV}}{m_{t}}+23\,\text{log}\frac{m_{t}}{m_{b}}+25\,\text{log}\frac{m_{b}}{m_{c}}+27\,\text{log}\frac{m_{c}}{\mu_{L}}+\frac{6\pi}{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{L})}

(53)

keeping
\alpha_{s}(1\text{TeV})
fixed gives

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-21+23+m_{t}\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}m_{t}}\frac{6\pi}{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{L})}\implies m_{t}\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}m_{t}}\frac{1}{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{L})}=-\frac{1}{3\pi}

(54)

{exercisenb}

In
\mathsf{d}=4
spacetime dimensions, work out the field content of Lorentz-invariant operators with dimension
\mathscr{D}
for
\mathscr{D}=1,\ldots,6
. At this point, do not try and work out which operators are independent, just the possible structure of allowed operators. Use the notation
\phi
for a scalar,
\psi
for a fermion,
X_{\mu\nu}
for a field strength, and
D
for a derivative. For example, an operator of type
\phi^{2}D
such as
\phi D_{\mu}\phi
is not allowed because it is not Lorentz-invariant. An operator of type
\phi^{2}D^{2}
could be either
D_{\mu}\phi D^{\mu}\phi
or
\phi D^{2}\phi
, so a
\phi^{2}D^{2}
operator is allowed, and we will worry later about how many independent
\phi^{2}D^{2}
operators can be constructed.

SOLUTION:

We use the notation
[A]=n
for the mass dimension of the operator A. The dimensionality of gauge boson, fermion and scalar fields in
d
spacetime dimensions can be determined from the kinetic terms in the Lagrangian imposing the condition

\left[\mathcal{L}\right]=d\,.

The three kinetic terms are (numerical constants are irrelevant for this discussion)

\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi\,,\qquad\bar{\psi}i\not{\partial}\psi\,,\qquad X_{\mu\nu}X^{\mu\nu}\,,

(55)

where
\phi
,
\psi
and
X
are the scalar field, the fermion field and the gauge boson field strength respectively. It follows immediately

\left[XX\right]=d\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,\left[X\right]=\frac{d}{2}\,,

(56)

for the field strength. For the scalar field we have

[\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi]=2[\partial]+2[\phi]=d\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,[\phi]=\frac{d-2}{2}\,,

(57)

while for the fermion field we have

[\bar{\psi}i\not{\partial}\psi]=[\partial]+2[\psi]=d\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,[\psi]=\frac{d-1}{2}\,.

(58)

In
d=4
we then have

[\phi]=1\,,\quad[D]=1\,,\quad[\psi]=\frac{3}{2}\,,\quad[X]=2\,.

(59)

The Lorentz-invariant operator structures of mass dimension
n
are:

•

n=1
:
\phi
;

•

n=2
:
\phi^{2}
,

•

n=3
:
\psi^{2}
,
\phi^{3}
;

•

n=4
:
\phi^{2}D^{2}
,
\phi^{4}
,
X^{2}
,
\phi\psi^{2}
,
\psi^{2}D
;

•

n=5
:
\psi^{2}D^{2}
,
\psi^{2}\phi^{2}
,
\psi^{2}X
,
\phi X^{2}
,
\phi^{5}
,
\phi^{3}D^{2}
,
\psi^{2}\phi D
;

•

n=6
:
\phi^{6}
,
\phi^{2}X^{2}
,
\phi^{2}D^{4}
,
D^{2}X^{2}
,
X^{3}
,
\psi^{4}
,
\psi^{2}D^{3}
,
X\phi^{2}D^{2}
,
\phi^{4}D^{2}
,
\psi^{2}\phi D^{2}
,
\psi^{2}\phi^{2}D
,
\psi^{2}X\phi
,
\psi^{2}\phi^{3}
.

D^{n}
for
n=2,4,6
are not allowed, because there must be at least one field for
D
to act on. For
n=0
, there is always the operator
\mathbbm{1}
.
D^{2}\phi
for
n=3
is a total derivative and integrates to zero.
D^{2}X
for
n=4
is
D_{\mu}D_{\nu}X^{\mu\nu}
,
and antisymmetry of
X
converts this to
\left[D_{\mu},D_{\nu}\right]X^{\mu\nu}\propto X^{2}
. Several operators, such as
\phi^{2}D^{4}
can be eliminated by field redefinitions. Similar simplifications occur in the next exercise.

{exercisenb}

For
\mathsf{d}=2,3,4,5,6
dimensions, work out the field content of operators with dimension
\mathscr{D}\leq\mathsf{d}
, i.e. the “renormalizable” operators.

SOLUTION:

Using the general result for mass dimensionality in the previous exercise, we can now work out Lorentz-invariant renormalizable operators in different spacetime dimensions.,
i.e.
those with dimension
n\leq d
. Note that at
n=d
there will be the kinetic term operators and these will always appear as
\phi^{2}D^{2}
,
\psi^{2}D
and
X^{2}
for scalar, fermion and gauge boson respectively. Except in the particular case of
n=2
, we will omit these operators in the lists. There is also the operator
\mathbbm{1}
(cosmological constant) which is also omitted in the lists.

•

d=2

[\phi]=0\,,\quad[D]=1\,,\quad[\psi]=\frac{1}{2}\,,\quad[X]=1\,.

(60)

In this particular case, the scalar field can enter with an arbitrary power
p
by itself at
n=0
or in other operators at
n=1,2
since it does not add any mass dimension to
n
and is Lorentz-invariant. For each class of operator we then have an infinite set of operators when we change the value of
p
. So we have

–

n=0
:
\phi^{p}
;

–

n=1
:
\phi^{p}\psi^{2}
;

–

n=2
:
\phi^{p}\psi^{4}
,
\phi^{p}D^{2}
,
\phi^{p}X^{2}
,
\phi^{p}\psi^{2}D
,
\psi^{2}X\phi^{p}
.

•

d=3

[\phi]=\frac{1}{2}\,,\quad[D]=1\,,\quad[\psi]=1\,,\quad[X]=\frac{3}{2}\,.

(61)

From now on there are no more non-trivial
n=0
operators.

–

n=1/2
:
\phi
;

–

n=1
:
\phi^{2}
;

–

n=3/2
:
\phi^{3}
;

–

n=2
:
\phi^{4}
,
\psi^{2}
;

–

n=5/2
:
\phi^{5}
,
\psi^{2}\phi
;

–

n=3
:
\phi^{6}
,
\phi^{2}\psi^{2}
;

•

d=4

[\phi]=1\,,\quad[D]=1\,,\quad[\psi]=\frac{3}{2}\,,\quad[X]=2\,.

(62)

This case has already been analyzed in the previous exercise, so we can skip it.

•

d=5

[\phi]=\frac{3}{2}\,,\quad[D]=1\,,\quad[\psi]=2\,,\quad[X]=\frac{5}{2}\,.

(63)

There are no more operator for
n=1
and from now on the only
n=2
operator is
D^{2}
, so we don’t write it anymore.

–

n=3/2
:
\phi
;

–

n=2
:
none
;

–

n=5/2
:
none
;

–

n=3
:
\phi^{2}
;

–

n=3/2
:
none
;

–

n=4
:
\psi^{2}
.

–

n=9/2
:
\phi^{3}
;

In this case, at
n=5
there are only the kinetic terms.

•

d=6

[\phi]=2\,,\quad[D]=1\,,\quad[\psi]=\frac{5}{2}\,,\quad[X]=3\,.

(64)

–

n=1
:
none
;

–

n=2
:
\phi
;

–

n=3
:
none
;

–

n=4
:
\phi^{2}
;

–

n=5
:
\psi^{2}
;

–

n=6
:
\phi^{3}
.

{exercisenb}

Compute the decay rate
\Gamma(b\to ce^{-}\overline{\nu}_{e})
with the interaction Lagrangian

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=-\frac{4G_{F}}{\sqrt{2}}V_{cb}(\overline{c}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}b)(\overline{\nu}_{e}\gamma_{\mu}P_{L}e)

with
m_{e}\to 0
,
m_{\nu}\to 0
, but retaining the dependence on
\rho=m_{c}^{2}/m_{b}^{2}
. It is convenient to write the three-body phase space in terms of the variables
x_{1}=2E_{e}/m_{b}
and
x_{2}=2E_{\nu}/m_{b}
.

SOLUTION:

At tree-level, the amplitude for the decay
b\to ce^{-}\bar{\nu}_{e}
is given by,

i\mathcal{A}=\frac{4G_{F}}{\sqrt{2}}V_{cb}\left(\bar{c}\gamma^{\mu}b_{L}\right)\left(e_{L}\gamma_{\mu}\bar{\nu}_{e}\right)

(65)

where we use the same symbol for the spinor fields and their polarization spinors
u(p,s)
.

To compute the square of the amplitude, first consider

\displaystyle\left(\bar{c}_{L}\gamma^{\mu}b_{L}\right)\left(\bar{c}_{L}\gamma^{\nu}b_{L}\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\left(\bar{c}_{L}\gamma^{\mu}b_{L}\right)\left(\bar{b}_{L}\gamma^{\nu}c_{L}\right)

(66)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\text{Tr}\left[\gamma^{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5})b\bar{b}\gamma^{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5})c\bar{c}\right]

(67)

Then performing a sum over the different available spin states,

\text{Tr}\left[\gamma^{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5})(\not{p}_{b}+m_{b})\gamma^{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5})(\not{p}_{c}+m_{c})\right]=T^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}p_{b}^{\alpha}p_{c}^{\beta}

(68)

\displaystyle T^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}=8\left(\eta^{\mu\alpha}\eta^{\nu\beta}-\eta^{\mu\nu}\eta^{\alpha\beta}+\eta^{\mu\beta}\eta^{\nu\alpha}+i\epsilon^{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}\right)\,.

(69)

The probability is then,

\left\langle\absolutevalue{\mathcal{A}}^{2}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\text{spins}}|i\mathcal{A}|^{2}=\frac{G_{F}^{2}\absolutevalue{V_{cb}}^{2}}{4}T^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}T^{\rho\sigma}_{\mu\nu}\,p_{b}^{\alpha}p_{c}^{\beta}p_{\nu,\rho}p_{e,\sigma}=64\,G_{F}^{2}\absolutevalue{V_{cb}}^{2}\;(p_{b}\cdot p_{\nu})\;(p_{c}\cdot p_{e})

(70)

summing over all spins, and including a
1/2
for spin-averaging over the initial
b
-quark spin.

The three body phase space is,

\displaystyle d\Pi_{3}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}_{c}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,2E_{c}}\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}_{e}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,2|\mathbf{p}_{e}|}\int\frac{d^{3}\vec{p}_{\nu}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,2|\vec{p}_{\nu}|}(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}({p}_{c}+{p}_{e}+{p}_{\nu}-p_{b})\,.

(71)

Note that when we integrate over the spin-summed probability, we are going to need the integral,

I^{\alpha\beta}(q)=\int\frac{d^{3}\vec{p}_{e}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,2|\vec{p}_{e}|}\int\frac{d^{3}\vec{p}_{\nu}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,2|\vec{p}_{\nu}|}(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}({p}_{e}+{p}_{\nu}-q)\;p_{e}^{\alpha}p_{\nu}^{\beta}

(72)

The simplest way to evaluate this integral is to exploit Lorentz invariance to write it as,

I^{\alpha\beta}(q)=\eta^{\alpha\beta}I_{1}+q^{\alpha}q^{\beta}I_{2}

(73)

and then to calculate the two independent Lorentz scalars
I_{1,2}
in the frame
\text{\boldmath$q$}=0
, and use

\displaystyle p_{e}\cdot p_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}q^{2}

\displaystyle q\cdot p_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}q^{2}

\displaystyle q\cdot p_{e}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}q^{2}

(74)

which follow from
q=p_{e}+p_{\nu}
,
p_{e}^{2}=p_{\nu}^{2}=0
.
Explicitly,

\displaystyle\eta_{\alpha\beta}I^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}_{e}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,2|\mathbf{p}_{e}|}\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}_{\nu}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,2|\mathbf{p}_{\nu}|}(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}({p}_{e}+{p}_{\nu}-q)\;(p_{e}\cdot p_{\nu})

(75)

The space
\delta
-function fixes
\mathbf{p}_{\nu}=-\mathbf{p}_{e}
, and
E_{\nu}=E_{e}=\absolutevalue{\mathbf{p}_e}
, giving

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{2}}{2\pi}\int\frac{\absolutevalue{\mathbf{p}_e}^{2}d\absolutevalue{\mathbf{p}_e}}{(2\absolutevalue{\mathbf{p}_e})(2\absolutevalue{\mathbf{p}_e})}\delta(q^{0}-2\absolutevalue{\mathbf{p}_e})=\frac{1}{16\pi}q^{2}

(76)

and

\displaystyle q_{\alpha}q_{\beta}I^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}_{e}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,2|\mathbf{p}_{e}|}\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}_{\nu}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,2|\mathbf{p}_{\nu}|}(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}({p}_{e}+{p}_{\nu}-q)\;(q\cdot p_{e})(q\cdot p_{\nu})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{32\pi}q^{4}

(77)

from eqn (
74
).
These give

I^{\alpha\beta}(q)=\frac{1}{96\pi}\left(\eta^{\alpha\beta}q^{2}+2q^{\alpha}q^{\beta}\right).

(78)

The differential decay rate is

d\Gamma(b\to ce^{-}\bar{\nu}_{e})=\frac{1}{2m_{b}}d\Pi_{3}\;\left\langle\absolutevalue{\mathcal{A}}^{2}\right\rangle

(79)

and the
\delta
-function in the three-body phase space eqn (
71
) can be written as

\displaystyle\delta^{(4)}({p}_{c}+{p}_{e}+{p}_{\nu}-p_{b})

\displaystyle=\int\text{d}^{4}q\ \delta^{(4)}({p}_{e}+{p}_{\nu}-q)\delta^{(4)}({p}_{c}+q-p_{b})\,.

(80)

This gives

\displaystyle d\Gamma(b\to ce^{-}\bar{\nu}_{e})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2m_{b}}\frac{64G_{F}^{2}\absolutevalue{V_{cb}}^{2}}{\pi}\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}_{c}}{(2\pi)^{3}2E_{c}}\int\text{d}^{4}q\ \delta^{(4)}({p}_{c}+q-p_{b})I^{\alpha\beta}(q)p_{c\alpha}p_{b\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{G_{F}^{2}\absolutevalue{V_{cb}}^{2}}{3\pi^{2}}\int\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}_{c}}{(2\pi)^{3}2E_{c}}\left[-4m_{b}E_{c}^{2}+3E_{c}(m_{b}^{2}+m_{c}^{2})-2m_{b}m_{c}^{2}\right]

(81)

The
\mathbf{p}_{c}
integral is

\displaystyle\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p}_{c}}{(2\pi)^{3}2E_{c}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\absolutevalue{\mathbf{p}_c}\text{d}E_{c}

(82)

Using the above results, we find,

\frac{d\Gamma(b\to ce^{-}\bar{\nu}_{e})}{dE_{c}}=|V_{cb}|^{2}\frac{G_{F}^{2}}{12\pi^{3}}\sqrt{E_{c}^{2}-m_{c}^{2}}\left[-4m_{b}E_{c}^{2}+3E_{c}(m_{b}^{2}+m_{c}^{2})-2m_{b}m_{c}^{2}\right]

(83)

The total decay rate is given by integrating between
E_{c}=m_{c}
and
E_{c}=(m_{c}^{2}+m_{b}^{2})/2m_{c}
, the maximum energy kinematically allowed,

\displaystyle\Gamma(b\to ce^{-}\bar{\nu}_{e})

\displaystyle=|V_{cb}|^{2}\frac{G_{F}^{2}m_{b}^{5}}{192\pi^{3}}\left(1-8\rho+8\rho^{3}-\rho^{4}-12\rho^{2}\log\,\rho\right)

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{c}^{2}}{m_{b}^{2}}

(84)

{exercisenb}

Compute the one-loop scalar graph Fig.
3
with a scalar of mass
m
and interaction vertex
-\lambda\phi^{4}/4!
in the
\overline{\text{MS}}
scheme. Verify the answer is of the form eqn (L5.15). The overall normalization will be different, because this exercise uses a real scalar field, and
H
in the SM is a complex scalar field.

Figure 3:
One loop correction to the scalar mass from the
-\lambda\phi^{4}/4!
interaction.

SOLUTION:

The Lagrangian is

\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\phi^{2}-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\phi^{4}\,.

(85)

The Feynman vertex for the self-interaction is
-i\lambda
(the
4!
factor is canceled by the
4!
different ways of combining the four
\phi
lines in the diagram).

Calling
k
the momentum running in the loop, we have (the graph has a symmetry factor of
1/2
)

I=-i\frac{1}{2}\lambda\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{\left[k^{2}-m^{2}\right]}=\frac{\lambda m^{2}}{2(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\frac{\Gamma(1-\frac{d}{2})}{\Gamma(1)}\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)^{2-\frac{d}{2}}\,.

(86)

Expanding it for
\epsilon\to 0
we have

I=\frac{\lambda m^{2}}{32\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\hat{\epsilon}}-\ln\frac{m^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)\,.

(87)

In the
\overline{\rm MS}
scheme the counterterm will cancel the
1/\hat{\epsilon}
pole, leaving

I+I_{\text{c.t.}}=-\frac{\lambda m^{2}}{32\pi^{2}}\ln\frac{m^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,.

(88)

The Higgs mass correction has a slightly different prefactor because
H
is a complex field, and because of a different normalization convention for the
(H^{\dagger}H)^{2}
vertex.

{exercisenb}

Compute
I_{F}
and
I_{\text{EFT}}
given in eqns (L5.19,L5.21) in dimensional regularization in
\mathsf{d}=4-2\epsilon
dimensions. Both integrals have UV divergences, and the
1/\epsilon
pieces are canceled by counterterms. Determine the counterterm contributions
I_{F,\text{ct}}
,
I_{\text{EFT},\text{ct}}
to the two integrals.

SOLUTION:

The full integral is given by,

\displaystyle I_{\text{F}}

\displaystyle=-i\mu^{2\epsilon}\int_{0}^{1}dx\,\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\frac{1}{(k^{2}-xm^{2}-(1-x)M^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\Gamma(\epsilon)\int_{0}^{1}dx\,\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{xm^{2}+(1-x)M^{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\int_{0}^{1}dx\,\log\left(\frac{\bar{\mu}^{2}}{xm^{2}+(1-x)M^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\frac{1}{M^{2}-m^{2}}\left(M^{2}\log\frac{M^{2}}{\mu^{2}}-m^{2}\log\frac{m^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\right)+1\right]

(89)

The full theory counterterm cancels the divergence,

\displaystyle I_{\text{F,c.t.}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon}

(90)

In an EFT expansion, we try to capture this answer by adding higher dimension operators, each suppressed by the scale
M
. This looks like,

\displaystyle I_{\text{EFT}}

\displaystyle=i\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{k^{2}-m^{2}}\,\frac{1}{M^{2}}\left[1+\frac{k^{2}}{M^{2}}+\frac{k^{4}}{M^{4}}+...\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}}\right)^{a+1}\,(-1)^{a+1}\frac{\Gamma(\epsilon-a-1)\Gamma(2+a-\epsilon)}{\Gamma(2-\epsilon)}\,\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}}\right)^{a+1}\,\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\gamma_{E}+1+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]\,\left[1+\epsilon\,\log\left(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\log\frac{\bar{\mu}^{2}}{m^{2}}\right]\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}}\right)^{a+1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\log\frac{m^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}+1\right]\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}-m^{2}}

(91)

Each term in the EFT expansion is UV divergent. Note that in the EFT, each of the operators has its own counterterm,

I_{\text{EFT,c.t.}}^{(n)}=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}\epsilon}\left(\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}}\right)^{n}

(92)

and so the anomalous dimensions in the two theories are different,

\gamma_{\text{Full}}\neq\gamma_{\text{EFT}}

which has important consequences for resumming large logarithms. The sum of all the counterterms (needed for Exercise 5.6) is

I_{\text{EFT,c.t.}}=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}\epsilon}\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}-m^{2}}

(93)

The resummed answer captures the correct analyticity structure in the IR scale
m
(i.e. the branch cut from the logarithm), but not the UV scale
M
. Note that the series only formally converges for the soft part of the integral,
\int_{0}^{M}dk
, which is the regime of validity of this EFT. In order to successfully match the full integral, we need to add an additional term, given by the “matching condition”,

I_{\text{Match}}=I_{\text{Full}}-I_{\text{EFT}}.

(94)

Mathematically, this corresponds to the remainder of the integration
\int_{M}^{\infty}dk
, and physically reflects the high energy modes which we have integrated out in going to the EFT description (see the next problem).

{exercisenb}

Compute
I_{M}\equiv\left(I_{F}+I_{F,\text{ct}}\right)-\left(I_{\text{EFT}}+I_{\text{EFT},\text{ct}}\right)
and show that it is analytic in
m
.

SOLUTION:

Using
I_{F}
and
I_{\text{EFT}}
from the previous solution, with the
1/\epsilon
pieces dropped,

\displaystyle I_{M}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\left[-\frac{1}{M^{2}-m^{2}}\left(M^{2}\log\frac{M^{2}}{\mu^{2}}-m^{2}\log\frac{m^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\right)+1\right]+\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\left[-\log\frac{m^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}+1\right]\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}-m^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\frac{M^{2}}{m^{2}-M^{2}}\left[\log\frac{M^{2}}{\mu^{2}}-1\right]\,.

(95)

The non-analytic
\log m^{2}
term has canceled, and
I_{M}
is analytic in
m
.

{exercisenb}

Compute
I_{F}^{(\text{exp})}
, i.e.
I_{F}
with the IR
m
scale expanded out

\displaystyle I_{F}^{(\text{exp})}

\displaystyle=-i\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{\text{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{(k^{2}-M^{2})}\left[\frac{1}{k^{2}}+\frac{m^{2}}{k^{4}}+\ldots\right]\,.

Note that the first term in the expansion has a
1/\epsilon
UV divergence, and the remaining terms have
1/\epsilon
IR divergences.

SOLUTION:

\displaystyle I_{F}^{(\text{exp})}

\displaystyle=-i\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{k^{2}-M^{2}}\frac{1}{k^{2}}\left[1+\frac{m^{2}}{k^{2}}+\frac{m^{4}}{k^{4}}+...\right]

(96)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\log\frac{M^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}+1\right]\frac{M^{2}}{M^{2}-m^{2}}

(97)

on integrating term-by-term and adding. The finite part agrees with eqn (
3
).

{exercisenb}

Compute
I_{F}^{(\text{exp})}+I_{F,\text{ct}}
using
I_{F,\text{ct}}
determined in Exercise
3
. Show that the UV divergence cancels, and the remaining
1/\epsilon
IR divergence is the same as the UV counterterm
I_{\text{EFT},ct}
in the EFT.

SOLUTION:

\displaystyle I_{F}^{(\text{exp})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\log\frac{M^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}+1\right]\frac{M^{2}}{M^{2}-m^{2}}

(98)

from Exercise 5.5 and

\displaystyle I_{\text{F,c.t.}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon}

(99)

from Exercise 5.3, so

\displaystyle I_{F}^{(\text{exp})}+I_{F,\text{ct}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\left\{\frac{1}{\epsilon}\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}-m^{2}}+\left[-\log\frac{M^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}+1\right]\frac{M^{2}}{M^{2}-m^{2}}\right\}

(100)

and the infinite part is the same as eqn (
93
).

{exercisenb}

Compute
I_{\text{EFT}}^{(\text{exp})}
, i.e.
I_{\text{EFT}}
with the IR
m
scale expanded out. Show that it is a scaleless integral which vanishes. Using the known UV divergence from Exercise
3
, write it in the form

\displaystyle I_{\text{EFT}}^{(\text{exp})}

\displaystyle=-B\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\text{UV}}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\text{IR}}}\right]\,,

and show that the IR divergence agrees with that in
I_{F}^{(\text{exp})}+I_{F,\text{ct}}
.

SOLUTION:

\displaystyle I_{\text{EFT}}^{(\text{exp})}

\displaystyle=-i\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{{\rm d}^{\mathsf{d}}k}{(2\pi)^{\mathsf{d}}}\ \left[\frac{1}{k^{2}}+\frac{m^{2}}{k^{4}}+\ldots\right]\left[-\frac{1}{M^{2}}-\frac{k^{2}}{M^{4}}-\ldots\right].

(101)

Multiplying out gives integrals of the form

\displaystyle-i\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{{\rm d}^{\mathsf{d}}k}{(2\pi)^{\mathsf{d}}}\ \frac{(m^{2})^{r}}{(M^{2})^{s}}(k^{2})^{2+s-r}

(102)

which are scaleless and vanish. From Exercise 5.3,

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}-m^{2}}.

(103)

and the
1/\epsilon_{\text{IR}}
divergent term agrees with eqn (
100
). [Note the various signs.]

\displaystyle I_{\text{EFT}}^{(\text{exp})}+I_{\text{EFT,c.t.}}^{(\text{exp})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\text{IR}}}B

(104)

We can also compute the integral directly. The log divergent terms are

\displaystyle I_{\text{EFT}}^{(\text{exp})}

\displaystyle=-i\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{{\rm d}^{\mathsf{d}}k}{(2\pi)^{\mathsf{d}}}\ \left[-\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}}-\frac{m^{4}}{M^{4}}-\ldots\right]\frac{1}{k^{4}}

(105)

Using eqn (L5.34) gives

\displaystyle I_{\text{EFT}}^{(\text{exp})}

\displaystyle=\left[-\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}}-\frac{m^{4}}{M^{4}}-\ldots\right]\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\text{UV}}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\text{IR}}}\right]

(106)

so that

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}}+\frac{m^{4}}{M^{4}}+\ldots\right]=\frac{m^{2}}{M^{2}-m^{2}}.

(107)

The
1/\epsilon_{\text{UV}}
terms are canceled by the counterterm eqn (
93
), and the remaining terms, which are IR divergent, agree with eqn (
100
).

{exercisenb}

Compute
\left(I_{F}^{(\text{exp})}+I_{F,\text{ct}}\right)-\left(I_{\text{EFT}}^{(\text{exp})}+I_{\text{EFT},\text{ct}}\right)
and show that all the
1/\epsilon
divergences (both UV and IR) cancel, and the result is equal to
I_{M}
found in Exercise
3
.

SOLUTION:

This follows immediately using eqn (
100
) and eqn (
104
).

{exercisenb}

Make sure you understand why you can compute
I_{M}
simply by taking
I_{F}^{(\text{exp})}
and dropping all
1/\epsilon
terms (both UV and IR).

SOLUTION:

No written solution needed.

{exercisenb}

Compute the QED on-shell electron form factors
F_{1}(q^{2})
and
F_{2}(q^{2})
expanded to first order in
q^{2}/m^{2}
using dimensional regularization to regulate the IR and UV divergences. This gives the one-loop matching to heavy-electron EFT. Note that it is much simpler to
first
expand and then do the Feynman parameter integrals. A more difficult version of the problem is to compute the on-shell quark form factors in QCD, which gives the one-loop matching to the HQET Lagrangian. For help with the computation, see Ref.
[
18
]
. Note that in the non-Abelian case, using background field gauge is helpful because the amplitude respects gauge invariance on the external gluon fields.

SOLUTION:

Figure 4:
Feynman diagram of 1-loop QED vertex correction, as calculated in Exercise
3
.

To calculate the QED electron on-shell form factors
F_{1}(q^{2})
and
F_{2}(q^{2})
up to one loop, we consider the vertex as pictured in Figure
4
. The form factors
F_{i}(q^{2})
are defined as

\bar{u}(p_{2})\Gamma^{\mu}(q^{2})u(p_{1})=\bar{u}(p_{2})\left(F_{1}(q^{2})\gamma^{\mu}+F_{2}(q^{2})\frac{i\sigma^{\mu\nu}q_{\nu}}{2m}\right)u(p_{1})

(108)

with
q=p_{2}-p_{1}
, where we have obviously
F_{1}(0)=1
and
F_{2}(0)=0
at tree level. We define
\tilde{F}_{i}(q^{2})
as the one-loop contribution to the full form factors
F_{i}(q^{2})
.

We will calculate the
\tilde{F}_{i}(q^{2})
by writing down the Feynman rules of Figure
4
, identify the integrals that will give us
\tilde{F}_{1}(q^{2})
and
\tilde{F}_{2}(q^{2})
, do the integrals over the loop momentum in
d
dimensions, expand in
\hat{q}^{2}=q^{2}/m^{2}
to first order and evaluate the leftover Feynman parameter integrals.

Applying the Feynman rules gives

\displaystyle-ie\Gamma^{\mu}(q^{2})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\left(-ie\gamma^{\nu}\right)\frac{i\left(\not{k}+\not{p}_{2}+m\right)}{\left(k+p_{2}\right)^{2}-m^{2}+i\epsilon}\left(-ie\gamma^{\mu}\right)\frac{i\left(\not{k}+\not{p}_{1}+m\right)}{\left(k+p_{1}\right)^{2}-m^{2}+i\epsilon}\left(-ie\gamma^{\rho}\right)\frac{-ig_{\nu\rho}}{k^{2}+i\epsilon}

\displaystyle=-e^{3}\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{\gamma^{\nu}\left(\not{k}+\not{p}_{2}+m\right)\gamma^{\mu}\left(\not{k}+\not{p}_{1}+m\right)\gamma_{\nu}}{\left[\left(k+p_{2}\right)^{2}-m^{2}+i\epsilon\right]\left[\left(k+p_{1}\right)^{2}-m^{2}+i\epsilon\right]\left[k^{2}+i\epsilon\right]}.

(109)

First, we want to massage the denominator. By introducing Feynman parameters, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{[\dots]}=\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1}dy\int_{0}^{1}dz\frac{2\,\delta(1-x-y-z)}{\left(y\left[\left(k+p_{2}\right)^{2}-m^{2}\right]+x\left[\left(k+p_{1}\right)^{2}-m^{2}\right]+zk^{2}\right)^{3}}.

(110)

The denominator can be simplified further (using
x+y+z=1
, overall momentum conservation
q+p_{1}=p_{2}
and on-shellness of the real electrons
p_{1}^{2}=p_{2}^{2}=m^{2}
) to

\displaystyle\left(\dots\right)^{3}

\displaystyle=\left(\left[k+xp_{1}+yp_{2}\right]^{2}-\left[xp_{1}+yp_{2}\right]^{2}+x(p_{1}^{2}-m^{2})+y(p_{2}^{2}-m^{2})\right)^{3}

\displaystyle=\left(\left[k+xp_{1}+yp_{2}\right]^{2}-x^{2}p_{1}^{2}+xy\left((p_{2}-p_{1})^{2}-p_{1}^{2}-p_{2}^{2}\right)-y^{2}p_{2}^{2}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle=\left(\left[k+xp_{1}+yp_{2}\right]^{2}+xyq^{2}-x(x+y)p_{1}^{2}-y(x+y)p_{2}^{2}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle=\left(\left[k+xp_{1}+yp_{2}\right]^{2}+xyq^{2}-(x+y)^{2}m^{2}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle=\left(l^{2}+xyq^{2}-(1-z)^{2}m^{2}\right)^{3},

(111)

with shifted momenta
l=k+xp_{1}+yp_{2}
, which replaces the integrations over loop momentum
k
.

The numerator of (
109
) can be reduced using the following identities of the Dirac
\gamma
matrices in
d
dimensions

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{\mu}

\displaystyle=d

(112)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}

\displaystyle=(2-d)\gamma^{\alpha}

(113)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\alpha}\gamma^{\beta}\gamma_{\mu}

\displaystyle=4g^{\alpha\beta}-(4-d)\gamma^{\alpha}\gamma^{\beta}

(114)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\alpha}\gamma^{\beta}\gamma^{\delta}\gamma_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-2\gamma^{\delta}\gamma^{\beta}\gamma^{\alpha}+(4-d)\gamma^{\alpha}\gamma^{\beta}\gamma^{\delta}.

(115)

We get

\displaystyle\gamma^{\nu}\dots\gamma_{\nu}

\displaystyle=-2m^{2}\gamma^{\mu}+4m(2k^{\mu}+p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu})-2(\not{k}+\not{p}_{1})\gamma^{\mu}(\not{k}+\not{p}_{2})

(116)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+(4-d)\left(\not{k}+\not{p}_{2}-m\right)\gamma^{\mu}\left(\not{k}+\not{p}_{1}-m\right).

By replacing
k
with parameter
l
as above (
k\rightarrow l-xp_{1}-yp_{2}
), and erasing the terms linear in
l
(note that we will eventually integrate over
l
and everything else is even in
l
), we get

\displaystyle\gamma^{\nu}\dots\gamma_{\nu}

\displaystyle=-2m^{2}\gamma^{\mu}+4m(p_{1}^{\mu}(1-2x)+p_{2}^{\mu}(1-2y))+(2-d)\not{l}\gamma^{\mu}\not{l}

(117)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-2(-y\not{p}_{2}+(1-x)\not{p}_{1})\gamma^{\mu}(-x\not{p}_{1}+(1-y)\not{p}_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+(4-d)\left(-x\not{p}_{1}+(1-y)\not{p}_{2}-m\right)\gamma^{\mu}\left((1-x)\not{p}_{1}-y\not{p}_{2}-m\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\text{terms linear in $l$}.

Using relations
q+p_{1}=p_{2}
and
1=x+y+z
, one gets

\displaystyle\gamma^{\nu}\dots\gamma_{\nu}

\displaystyle=-2m^{2}\gamma^{\mu}+4mz(p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu})+4m(x-y)q^{\mu}+(2-d)\not{l}\gamma^{\mu}\not{l}

(118)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-2(z\not{p}_{2}-(1-x)\not{q})\gamma^{\mu}(z\not{p}_{1}+(1-y)\not{q})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+(4-d)\left(x\not{q}+z\not{p}_{2}-m\right)\gamma^{\mu}\left(z\not{p}_{1}-y\not{q}-m\right).

Using on-shellness and completeness relations (we always have
\bar{u}(p_{2})\Gamma^{\mu}(q^{2})u(p_{1})
and therefore can write
\not{p}_{2}\,A=m\,A
as well as
A\,\not{p}_{1}=A\,m
for any
A
), we get for the numerator (after some rather uninspiring and tedious work)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\nu}\dots\gamma_{\nu}

\displaystyle=(2-d)\not{l}\gamma^{\mu}\not{l}+4mz(p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu})

(119)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+mq^{\mu}(x-y)\left(4-2z-(4-d)(1-z)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+m^{2}\gamma^{\mu}\left(-2-2z^{2}+(4-d)(1-z)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-q^{2}\gamma^{\mu}\left(2(z+xy)-(4-d)xy\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+im\sigma^{\mu\nu}q_{\nu}\left(2z(1+z)-(4-d)(1-z)^{2}\right).

In the first line, we can apply the Gordon identity
p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu}=2m\gamma^{\mu}-i\sigma^{\mu\nu}q_{\nu}
and make use of the relation
\not{l}\gamma^{\mu}\not{l}=\gamma^{\alpha}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\beta}g_{\alpha\beta}\frac{l^{2}}{d}=(2-d)\gamma^{\mu}\frac{l^{2}}{d}
to get

\displaystyle\gamma^{\nu}\dots\gamma_{\nu}

\displaystyle=(2-d)^{2}\gamma^{\mu}\frac{l^{2}}{d}

(120)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+mq^{\mu}(x-y)\left(4-2z-(4-d)(1-z)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+m^{2}\gamma^{\mu}\left(8z-2-2z^{2}+(4-d)(1-z)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-q^{2}\gamma^{\mu}\left(2(z+xy)-(4-d)xy\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-im\sigma^{\mu\nu}q_{\nu}(1-z)\left(2z+(4-d)(1-z)\right).

Obviously, the denominator (
111
) and the delta function is invariant under parameter exchange
x\leftrightarrow y
. Therefore, the second line of the numerator (
120
) gives zero contribution, as it changes sign under the same exchange.

Comparing to (
108
), the only contribution to
\tilde{F}_{2}(q^{2})
comes from the last line in (
120
), while lines 1, 3 and 4 give contributions to
\tilde{F}_{1}(q^{2})
. We can finally write down the one-loop form factors as

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{i}(q^{2})

\displaystyle=-2ie^{2}\mu^{4-d}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1}dy\int_{0}^{1}dz\int\frac{d^{d}l}{(2\pi)^{d}}\delta(1-x-y-z)\frac{A_{i}}{\left(l^{2}+xyq^{2}-(1-z)^{2}m^{2}\right)^{3}}

(121)

with

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2-d)^{2}l^{2}}{d}-(d-2)\left((1-z)^{2}m^{2}+xyq^{2}\right)+2z\left(2m^{2}-q^{2}\right)

(122)

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=-2m^{2}(1-z)\left(2z+(4-d)(1-z)\right).

(123)

We encounter two types of phase space integrals in
d
dimensions, namely

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{d}l}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{l^{2}}{\left(l^{2}-\Delta\right)^{3}}\text{, and}

(124)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{d}l}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{\left(l^{2}-\Delta\right)^{3}}.

(125)

It is worth to point out, that integrals of the form
\mathcal{I}_{2}
are not divergent in
d=4
dimensions. To be consistent, we will still keep the dependency on
d
explicit. This will help us to evaluate the Feynman parameter integrals, some of which diverge in
d=4
dimensions. We will not go through the calculation of the integrals
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
step by step, as it is basically following the standard recipe of Wick rotating, parametrizing the loop momentum
l
in
d
dimensional polar coordinates, identifying the
d-1
angular integrations as the surface of the
d
dimensional unit sphere (as we have only dependence on the radius of
l
) and computing the radial integration. We get the integrals to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}=\frac{d}{4}\frac{i}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{1}{\Delta^{2-d/2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{4-d}{2}\right)

(126)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}=\frac{-i}{2(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{1}{\Delta^{3-d/2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{6-d}{2}\right).

(127)

Let us now calculate
\tilde{F}_{1}(q^{2})
. From (
126
) and (
127
) as well as using the
\delta
-function for the integration over
z
, we get (
121
) with (
122
) to become

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{1}(q^{2})=

\displaystyle-2ie^{2}\mu^{4-d}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{(2-d)^{2}i}{4(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{1}{\left((x+y)^{2}m^{2}-xyq^{2}\right)^{2-d/2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{4-d}{2}\right)

\displaystyle-2ie^{2}\mu^{4-d}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{-i}{2(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{(2-d)\left((x+y)^{2}m^{2}+xyq^{2}\right)}{\left((x+y)^{2}m^{2}-xyq^{2}\right)^{3-d/2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{6-d}{2}\right)

\displaystyle-2ie^{2}\mu^{4-d}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{-i}{2(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{2(1-x-y)\left(2m^{2}-q^{2}\right)}{\left((x+y)^{2}m^{2}-xyq^{2}\right)^{3-d/2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{6-d}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2e^{2}\frac{(2-d)^{2}}{4(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{\mu^{4-d}}{m^{4-d}}\Gamma\left(\frac{4-d}{2}\right)\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{1}{\left((x+y)^{2}-xy\hat{q}^{2}\right)^{2-d/2}}

\displaystyle-2e^{2}\frac{(2-d)}{2(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{\mu^{4-d}}{m^{4-d}}\Gamma\left(\frac{6-d}{2}\right)\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{\left((x+y)^{2}+xy\hat{q}^{2}\right)}{\left((x+y)^{2}-xy\hat{q}^{2}\right)^{3-d/2}}

\displaystyle-2e^{2}\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{\mu^{4-d}}{m^{4-d}}\Gamma\left(\frac{6-d}{2}\right)\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{(1-x-y)\left(2-\hat{q}^{2}\right)}{\left((x+y)^{2}-xy\hat{q}^{2}\right)^{3-d/2}}.

(128)

Along the way, we introduced
\hat{q}^{2}=q^{2}/m^{2}
. Before we calculate the leftover integrals over
x
and
y
, we now want to expand the integrands in the heavy-electron-limit
\hat{q}^{2}\approx 0
to order
\hat{q}^{2}
. These expansions read

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(a-b\hat{q}^{2})^{2-d/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a^{2-d/2}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{b(d-4)}{a^{3-d/2}}\hat{q}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\hat{q}^{4})

(129)

\displaystyle\frac{a+b\hat{q}^{2}}{(a-b\hat{q}^{2})^{3-d/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a^{2-d/2}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{b(d-8)}{a^{3-d/2}}\hat{q}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\hat{q}^{4})

(130)

\displaystyle\frac{c-\hat{q}^{2}}{(a-b\hat{q}^{2})^{3-d/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{a^{3-d/2}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{bc(d-6)+2a}{a^{4-d/2}}\hat{q}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\hat{q}^{4}).

(131)

Therefore, we can solve the Feynman parameter integrals of the first line in (
128
)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{1}{\left(\dots\right)^{2-d/2}}\approx

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{1}{(x+y)^{4-d}}-\frac{xy(d-4)}{2(x+y)^{6-d}}\hat{q}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d-2}-\frac{d-4}{12(d-2)}\hat{q}^{2},

(132)

the one in the second line as

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{(\dots)}{\left(\dots\right)^{3-d/2}}\approx

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{1}{(x+y)^{4-d}}-\frac{xy(d-8)}{2(x+y)^{6-d}}\hat{q}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d-2}-\frac{d-8}{12(d-2)}\hat{q}^{2},

(133)

while the Feynman parameter integrals in the last line of (
128
) reads

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{(\dots)(\dots)}{(\dots)^{3-d/2}}\approx

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\Bigg[\frac{2(1-x-y)}{(x+y)^{6-d}}

\displaystyle\hskip 79.6678pt-\frac{(1-x-y)(xy(d-6)+(x+y)^{2})}{(x+y)^{8-d}}\hat{q}^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{2-\hat{q}^{2}}{(d-3)(d-4)}-\frac{d-6}{6(d-3)(d-4)}\hat{q}^{2}.

(134)

Note, that the last integral is divergent in
d=4
dimensions, which is the reason why we did the phase-space integral in
d
dimensions.

We will now put the solutions (
132
), (
133
) and (
134
) in (
128
). In the same step, we set the dimensions to
d=4-2\epsilon
and expand to order
\epsilon^{0}
. The result (with
\bar{\mu}^{2}=4\pi e^{-\gamma_{E}}\mu^{2}
) turns out to be

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{1}(\hat{q}^{2})=\frac{e^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{9-2\hat{q}^{2}}{3\epsilon}+\frac{8-\hat{q}^{2}}{2}-\frac{9-2\hat{q}^{2}}{3}\ln\left(\frac{m^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)+\mathcal{O}(\hat{q}^{4}).

(135)

The wave function graph Fig.
2
gives

\displaystyle\delta Z

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\left(\frac{3}{\epsilon}+4-3\log\frac{m^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\right)

(136)

Adding the tree-level contribution, and subtracting
\delta Z
, we have

\displaystyle F_{1}(\hat{q}^{2})=1+\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\left(-\frac{2\hat{q}^{2}}{3\epsilon}-\frac{\hat{q}^{2}}{2}+\frac{2\hat{q}^{2}}{3}\ln\left(\frac{m^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}(\hat{q}^{4},\epsilon).

(137)

Now, we will calculate
\tilde{F}_{2}(q^{2})
in the same manner. This will be very straight-forward as the procedure is exactly the same as in the calculation of
\tilde{F}_{1}(q^{2})
. From (
121
) with (
123
), we have

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{2}(q^{2})

\displaystyle=-2ie^{2}\mu^{4-d}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1}dy\int_{0}^{1}dz\int\frac{d^{d}l}{(2\pi)^{d}}

(138)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\delta(1-x-y-z)\frac{-2m^{2}(1-z)(2z+(4-d)(1-z))}{\left(l^{2}+xyq^{2}-(1-z)^{2}m^{2}\right)^{3}}.

We can use (
127
) for the phase-space integration and use the
\delta
-function for the integration over
z

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{2}(q^{2})

\displaystyle=-2ie^{2}\mu^{4-d}\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{-i}{2(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{-2m^{2}(x+y)(2(1+x+y)-d(x+y))}{((x+y)^{2}m^{2}-xyq^{2})^{3-d/2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{6-d}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=2e^{2}\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{\mu^{4-d}}{m^{4-d}}\Gamma\left(\frac{6-d}{2}\right)\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{2(x+y)+(2-d)(x+y)^{2}}{((x+y)^{2}-xy\hat{q}^{2})^{3-d/2}},

(139)

where we again introduced
\hat{q}^{2}=q^{2}/m^{2}
. The expansion of the integrand reads

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(a-b\hat{q}^{2})^{3-d/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a^{3-d/2}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{b(d-6)}{a^{4-d/2}}\hat{q}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\hat{q}^{4}),

(140)

and therefore we may write the leftover Feynman parameter integrations of
\tilde{F}_{2}(q^{2})
as follows

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\frac{\dots}{(\dots)^{3-d/2}}\approx

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}dx\int_{0}^{1-x}dy\Bigg[\frac{2}{(x+y)^{5-d}}+\frac{(2-d)}{(x+y)^{4-d}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\left(\frac{xy(d-6)}{(x+y)^{7-d}}+\frac{(2-d)xy(d-6)}{2(x+y)^{6-d}}\right)\hat{q}^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{d-3}-1-\frac{d-6}{6(d-3)}\hat{q}^{2}+\frac{d-6}{12}\hat{q}^{2}.

(141)

We end the calculation by injecting (
141
) into (
139
), set
d=4-2\epsilon
and expand to order
\epsilon^{0}
:

\displaystyle F_{2}(\hat{q}^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\left(2+\frac{1}{3}\hat{q}^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)+\mathcal{O}(\hat{q}^{4})

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\left(2+\frac{1}{3}\hat{q}^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\hat{q}^{4},\epsilon)

(142)

which is finite.

{exercisenb}

The SCET matching for the vector current
\overline{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi
for the Sudakov form factor is a variant of the previous problem. Compute
F_{1}(q^{2})
for on-shell massless quarks, in pure dimensional regularization with
Q^{2}=-q^{2}\not=0
. Here
Q^{2}
is the big scale, whereas in the previous problem
q^{2}
was the small scale. The spacelike calculation
Q^{2}>0
avoids having to deal with the
+i0^{+}
terms in the Feynman propagator which lead to imaginary parts. The timelike result can then be obtained by analytic continuation.

SOLUTION:

Figure 5:
The Feynman diagram encoding the virtual 1-loop correction to the vector current
\overline{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi
.

In SCET, the IR scales in which the full theory integral has to be expanded in are
m^{2}\sim\lambda^{2}
and
p^{2}\sim\lambda^{2}
, where
\lambda
is the small power counting parameter in SCET. Consequently, expanding the integrand in the IR scales leads to the massless version of the full calculation.

Using standard QCD Feynman rules for the diagram in Fig.
5
, describing the one-loop correction of the vector current
\overline{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi
, we get

\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=-i\,(4\pi)\,\alpha_{s}\,C_{F}\,\bar{u}(p_{2},s_{2})\,\tilde{\mu}^{2\varepsilon}\int\frac{\differential^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{\gamma^{\alpha}(\not{p}_{2}-\not{k})\gamma^{\mu}(\not{p}_{1}-\not{k})\gamma_{\alpha}}{(k^{2}-2\,p_{2}\cdot k)(k^{2}-2\,p_{1}\cdot k)k^{2}}\,v(-p_{1},s_{2})\,,

(143)

where
p_{i}^{2}=0
was used when multiplying out the propagators, and the usual
+i0^{+}
prescription is implied.

After introducing standard Feynman parameters for the three propagators, i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(k^{2}-2\,p_{2}\cdot k)(k^{2}-2\,p_{1}\cdot k)k^{2}}

(144)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{1}\differential x_{1}\int_{0}^{1-x_{1}}\differential x_{2}\,\frac{1}{(x_{2}k^{2}-2\,x_{2}p_{2}\cdot k+x_{1}k^{2}-2\,x_{1}p_{1}\cdot k+(1-x_{1}-x_{2})k^{2})^{3}}

(145)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{1}\differential x_{1}\int_{0}^{1-x_{1}}\differential x_{2}\,\frac{1}{(k^{2}-2\,k\cdot(x_{1}p_{1}+x_{2}p_{2}))^{3}}

(146)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{1}\differential x_{1}\int_{0}^{1-x_{1}}\differential x_{2}\,\frac{1}{((k-(x_{1}p_{1}+x_{2}p_{2}))^{2}-x_{1}x_{2}Q^{2})^{3}}\,,

(147)

and using
p_{1}\cdot p_{2}=1/2\,Q^{2}
, we apply a shift in the loop momentum
k\equiv\ell+x_{1}p_{1}+x_{2}p_{2}
. Consequently the denominator reduces to
(\ell^{2}-x_{1}x_{2}Q^{2})^{3}
, with
\ell
the new loop momentum.

Next we simplify the Dirac structure in the numerator. Applying the standard identity
\gamma^{\alpha}\gamma^{\beta}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\delta}\gamma_{\alpha}=-2\gamma^{\delta}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\beta}+(4-d)\gamma^{\beta}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\delta}
, and using that
\bar{u}(p_{2},s_{2})\not{p}_{2}=0
and
\not{p}_{1}v(-p_{1},s_{1})=0
in the massless case, we get

\mathcal{N}^{\mu}\equiv\gamma^{\alpha}(\not{p}_{2}-\not{k})\gamma^{\mu}(\not{p}_{1}-\not{k})\gamma_{\alpha}\cong-2(\not{p}_{1}-\not{k})\gamma^{\mu}(\not{p}_{2}-\not{k})+(4-d)\not{k}\gamma^{\mu}\not{k}\,,

(148)

where
\cong
means that the equality is valid only when sandwiched between the spinors and under the loop integral. The loop momentum shift from above leads to (again using the spinors on the left and right)

\mathcal{N}^{\mu}\cong-2((1-x_{1})\not{p}_{1}-\not{\ell})\gamma^{\mu}((1-x_{2})\not{p}_{2}-\not{\ell})+(4-d)(\not{\ell}+x_{1}\not{p}_{1})\gamma^{\mu}(\not{\ell}+x_{2}\not{p}_{2})\,.

(149)

Reconsidering the denominator of the loop integral, we notice that the dependence is only quadratic in
\ell
, such that in the numerator all terms linear in
\ell
evaluate to zero when integrated over due to symmetry. Consequently

\mathcal{N}^{\mu}\cong(2-d)\not{\ell}\gamma^{\mu}\not{\ell}-2(1-x_{1})(1-x_{2})\not{p}_{1}\gamma^{\mu}\not{p}_{2}+(4-d)x_{1}x_{2}\not{p}_{1}\gamma^{\mu}\not{p}_{2}\,.

(150)

The two remaining Dirac structures can furthermore be rewritten as

\not{p}_{1}\gamma^{\mu}\not{p}_{2}\cong-\gamma^{\mu}\not{p}_{1}\not{p}_{2}\cong-2\,\gamma^{\mu}p_{1}\cdot p_{2}=-\gamma^{\mu}Q^{2}\,,

(151)

and

\not{\ell}\gamma^{\mu}\not{\ell}=\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\ell^{\alpha}\ell^{\beta}\cong\frac{1}{d}\,\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\ell^{2}g^{\alpha\beta}=\gamma^{\mu}\frac{2-d}{d}\ell^{2}\,,

(152)

where we used the identity
\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\alpha}=(2-d)\gamma^{\mu}
and the fact that due to the Lorentz structure of the loop integral we can rewrite
\ell^{\alpha}\ell^{\beta}\cong 1/d\,\ell^{2}g^{\alpha\beta}
under the integral sign.

Ultimately, we arrive at

\mathcal{N}^{\mu}\cong\gamma^{\mu}\left[\frac{(2-d)^{2}}{d}\ell^{2}+Q^{2}\left(2(1-x_{1})(1-x_{2})-(4-d)x_{1}x_{2}\right)\right]\,.

(153)

The loop integral can now be solved by using the standard formulas

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}^{2\varepsilon}\int\frac{\differential^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{(\ell^{2}-x_{1}x_{2}Q^{2})^{3}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i\tilde{\mu}^{2\varepsilon}}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{\Gamma(3-d/2)}{\Gamma(3)}(x_{1}x_{2}Q^{2})^{d/2-3}\,,

(154)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}^{2\varepsilon}\int\frac{\differential^{d}\ell}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{\ell^{2}}{(\ell^{2}-x_{1}x_{2}Q^{2})^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{i\tilde{\mu}^{2\varepsilon}}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{\Gamma(1+d/2)\Gamma(2-d/2)}{\Gamma(d/2)\Gamma(3)}(x_{1}x_{2}Q^{2})^{d/2-2}\,,

(155)

and subsequently the Feynman parameter integrals by using

\int_{0}^{1}\differential x_{1}\int_{0}^{1-x_{1}}\differential x_{2}\,x_{1}^{a}x_{2}^{b}=\frac{\Gamma(1+a)\Gamma(1+b)}{\Gamma(3+a+b)}\,.

(156)

After putting everything together and a few simplifications one can write the final result as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\bar{u}(p_{1},s_{1})\gamma^{\mu}v(p_{2},s_{2})\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{F}}{4\pi}\frac{2^{-1+4\varepsilon}\,\pi^{1/2+\varepsilon}\,(\varepsilon(1-2\varepsilon)-2)\,\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)\Gamma(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon\,\Gamma(3/2-\varepsilon)}\left(\frac{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}{Q^{2}}\right)^{\varepsilon}

(157)

\displaystyle=\bar{u}(p_{1},s_{1})\gamma^{\mu}v(p_{2},s_{2})\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{F}}{4\pi}\bigg[-\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(-2\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{Q^{2}}-3\right)-8+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}-3\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{Q^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad-\log^{2}\frac{\mu^{2}}{Q^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\bigg]\,,

(158)

where
d=4-2\varepsilon
and
\tilde{\mu}^{2\varepsilon}=\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon\gamma_{E}}(4\pi)^{-\varepsilon}\mu^{2\varepsilon}
.

Dropping the
1/\varepsilon^{n}
terms (and the spinor structure) we obtain the SCET matching coefficient for the vector current

C(\mu^{2},Q^{2})=1+\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{F}}{4\pi}\left[-8+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}-3\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{Q^{2}}-\log^{2}\frac{\mu^{2}}{Q^{2}}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})\,.

(159)

{exercisenb}

Compute the SCET matching for timelike
q^{2}
, by analytically continuing the previous result. Be careful about the sign of the imaginary parts.

SOLUTION:

Remembering that
Q^{2}\equiv-q^{2}-i0^{+}
and
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\log(-1+i \epsilon)=\mathrm{sgn}\,(\epsilon)i\pi
we can rewrite
