H=\dot{a}/a
and so
H^{2}=8\pi G\rho_{0}/3
.

For a matter-dominated universe, for which
\rho_{0}\simeq\rho_{m0}\propto 1/a^{3}
and
a\propto t^{2/3}
show that as
c_{s}{\bf k}\to 0
eq (
1
) gives power-law solutions of the form
\delta_{0}\propto t^{n}
with
n=\frac{2}{3}
or
n=-1
. (The growing mode verifies the claim in class that
\delta_{0}\propto a
during matter domination.)

Consider now the transition between radiation and matter domination, for which
\rho_{0}=\rho_{m0}+\rho_{r0}
and so

H^{2}(a)=\frac{8\pi G\rho_{0}}{3}=\frac{H_{\rm eq}^{2}}{2}\left[\left(\frac{a_{\rm eq}}{a}\right)^{3}+\left(\frac{a_{\rm eq}}{a}\right)^{4}\right]\,,

(2)

where radiation-matter equality occurs when
a=a_{\rm eq}
, at which point
H(a=a_{\rm eq})=H_{\rm eq}
. The matter part of this expansion comes from

H_{m}^{2}:=\frac{8\pi G\rho_{m0}}{3}=\frac{H_{\rm eq}^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{a_{\rm eq}}{a}\right)^{3}\,.

(3)

Verify that
\delta_{0}(x)
satisfies

2x(1+x)\,\delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}+(3x+2)\,\delta_{0}^{\prime}-3\,\delta_{0}=0\,,

(4)

where the scale factor,
x=a/a_{\rm eq}
, is used as a proxy for time and primes denote differentiation with respect to
x
. Show that this is solved by
\delta_{0}\propto\left(x+\frac{2}{3}\right)
, and thereby show how the growing mode during matter domination does not grow during radiation domination. (Bonus: show that the linearly independent solutions to this one only grow logarithmically with
x
deep in the radiation-dominated era, for which
x\ll 1
.)

SOLUTION
: Slow growth of fluctuations during radiation domination

\ddot{\delta}_{\mathbf{k}}+2H\dot{\delta}_{\mathbf{k}}+\left(\frac{c_{s}^{2}\mathbf{k}^{2}}{a^{2}}-4\pi G\rho_{m0}\right)\delta_{\mathbf{k}}=0

(5)

In a matter dominated Universe, we can use the Friedmann equation,
3H^{2}=8\pi G\rho_{0}
, to write the evolution for long wavelength modes as,

\ddot{\delta}_{0}+2H\dot{\delta}_{0}-\frac{3}{2}H^{2}\delta_{0}=0

(6)

Changing the dependent variable to
a(t)
, we can use,
da=aHdt
and
H(t)=H_{0}a^{-3/2}
to write,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=H_{0}a^{-1/2}(H_{0}a^{-1/2}\delta_{0}^{\prime})^{\prime}+2H_{0}^{2}a^{-2}\delta_{0}^{\prime}-\frac{3}{2}H_{0}^{2}a^{-3}\delta_{0}

(7)

\displaystyle=H_{0}^{2}a^{-3}\left(a^{2}\partial_{a}^{2}+\frac{3}{2}a\partial_{a}-\frac{3}{2}\right)\delta_{0}

(8)

\displaystyle=H^{2}\sum_{n}\left(n+\frac{3}{2}\right)\left(n-1\right)\delta_{0}^{(n)}a^{n}

(9)

where we’ve written
\delta_{0}(a)
as
\sum_{n}\delta_{0}^{(n)}a^{n}
, and can conclude that the general solution is,

\delta_{0}(t)=c_{1}a+c_{2}a^{-3/2}\propto c_{1}t^{2/3}+c_{2}t^{-1}

(10)

where
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
are constants of integration (c.f. method of Frobenius).

Now consider,

H^{2}(a)=\frac{H_{\rm eq}^{2}}{2}\left[x^{-3}+x^{-4}\right],\;\;\;\;4\pi G\rho_{m0}=\frac{3H_{\rm eq}^{2}}{4}x^{-3}

(11)

where
x=a/a_{\rm eq}
is the dependent variable, and so we have,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{H_{\rm eq}^{2}}{2}\left[\frac{\sqrt{1+x}}{x}\left(\frac{\sqrt{1+x}}{x}\delta_{0}^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}+2\frac{1+x}{x^{2}}\delta_{0}^{\prime}-\frac{3}{2x^{3}}\delta_{0}\right]

(12)

\displaystyle=\frac{H_{\rm eq}^{2}}{4x^{3}}\left[2x(1+x)\partial_{x}^{2}+(3x+2)\partial_{x}-3\right]\delta_{0}

(13)

\displaystyle=2H_{m}^{2}\sum_{n}\left((2n+3)(n-1)\delta_{0}^{(n)}+2n^{2}\delta_{0}^{(n-1)}\right)

(14)

One solution to this is,
\delta\propto(x+2/3)
. During radiation domination, we have
x<1
, and so the constant
2/3
piece of the
\delta_{0}
fluctuations is important.

The other linearly independent solution is,

\delta_{0}=3y-\left(1+\frac{3x}{2}\right)\text{log}\frac{1+y}{1-y},\;\;\;\;y=\sqrt{1+x}

(15)

which grows like
\text{log}(x)
for
x\ll 1
.

Figure 13
:
The two independent solutions for growth of long wavelength fluctuations,
\delta_{0}(t)
.

{exercisenb}

Calculation of vacuum energy for a scalar field in a static spacetime

There are a variety of ways commonly used to compute quantum corrections to the vacuum energy, and this tutorial is meant to show how they are related. For the purposes of the exercise a free real scalar field is used, with action
S=\int{\hbox{d}}t\;L=\int{\hbox{d}}^{4}x\;{\mathcal{}L}
and Lagrangian
L=\int{\hbox{d}}^{3}x\;{\mathcal{}L}
. The Lagrangian density is

{\mathcal{}L}=-\sqrt{-g}\;\left[\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{\mu}\phi\,\partial^{\mu}\phi+\frac{1}{2}\,m^{2}\,\phi^{2}\right]\,,

(16)

and the resulting field equation is the Klein-Gordon equation

(-\Box+m^{2})\phi=(-g^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}+m^{2})\phi=0\,.

(17)

But the relationship between the calculations described below is more general than just for this one example.

Canonical calculation
The simplest approach to calculating the vacuum energy is the same calculation that identifies all of the energy eigenstates and eigenvalues. This starts by assuming a static background spacetime with metric
{\hbox{d}}s^{2}=-{\hbox{d}}t^{2}+g_{ij}\,{\hbox{d}}x^{i}\,{\hbox{d}}x^{j}
, for which
g_{ij}
is time-independent and a conserved energy can be formulated. Using the above action the field’s canonical momentum is

\pi(x)=\frac{\delta S}{\delta\dot{\phi}(x)}=\sqrt{-g}\;\dot{\phi}(x)\,,

(18)

(where an over-dot as in
\dot{\phi}
denotes
\partial_{t}
) and so the Hamiltonian density is

{\mathcal{}H}=\pi\,\dot{\phi}-{\mathcal{}L}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{2\sqrt{-g}}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{-g}\;\Bigl[g^{ij}\,\nabla_{i}\phi\,\nabla_{j}\phi+m^{2}\,\phi^{2}\Bigr]\,.

(19)

Background about quantization and mode functions

Because this is quadratic in the fields it is essentially a fancy harmonic oscillator. To diagonalize it we expand the fields in terms of creation and annihilation operators

\phi(x)=\sum_{n}\Bigl[a_{n}\,U_{n}(x)+a_{n}^{\star}\,U^{*}_{n}(x)\Bigr]\,,

(20)

where we choose the mode functions,
U_{n}(x)
, to be simultaneous eigenstates of
-g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}
and
i\partial_{t}
. That is they satisfy the Klein-Gordan equation,
(-\Box+m^{2})U_{n}=0
, in a basis that also satisfies

-g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}U_{n}(x)=\omega_{n}^{2}U_{n}(x)\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad i\dot{U}_{n}=\varepsilon_{n}\,U_{n}(x)\,,

(21)

for eigenvalues
\omega_{n}^{2}
and
\varepsilon_{n}
. The Klein-Gordon equation imposes a relation between these eigenvalues since
-\Box U_{n}=\ddot{U}_{n}-g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}U_{n}=(-\varepsilon_{n}^{2}-g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j})U_{n}
and so

\Bigl(-g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}+m^{2}\Bigr)U_{n}=(\omega_{n}^{2}+m^{2})U_{n}=\varepsilon_{n}^{2}\,U_{n}\,.

(22)

This shows how
\varepsilon_{n}^{2}=\omega_{n}^{2}+m^{2}
gets determined by the spectrum of
g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}
for the spacetime of interest.

So we may write

U_{n}({\bf x},t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{n}}}\;u_{n}({\bf x})\,e^{-i\varepsilon_{n}t}\,,

(23)

where the prefactor is chosen for later convenience. Similarly

\pi(x)=\sqrt{-g}\;\dot{\phi}=-i\sqrt{-g}\;\sum_{n}\varepsilon_{n}\Bigl[a_{n}\,U_{n}(x)-a_{n}^{\star}\,U^{*}_{n}(x)\Bigr]\,.

(24)

The covariant normalization condition for the modes is defined using the Wronskian by

\displaystyle W_{\Sigma}(U_{n},U_{m})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-i\int_{\Sigma}{\hbox{d}}^{3}{\bf x}\sqrt{-g}\;\Bigl[\dot{U}_{n}^{*}(x)\,U_{m}(x)-U_{n}^{*}(x)\,\dot{U}_{m}(x)\Bigr]

(26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}{\hbox{d}}^{3}{\bf x}\sqrt{-g}\;\Bigl[\varepsilon_{n}U_{n}^{*}\,U_{m}+\varepsilon_{m}U_{n}^{*}\,U_{m}\Bigr]=\delta_{mn}\,,

where
\Sigma
is a slice of fixed
t
. Similarly, because (
21
) tell us
i\dot{U}_{n}^{*}=-\varepsilon_{n}U_{n}^{*}
, we see that
U_{n}^{*}
and
U_{n}
are eigenstates for different energy eigenvalues (notice for
m\neq 0
there are no zero eigenvalues), and so are also orthogonal

\displaystyle W_{\Sigma}(U_{n}^{*},U_{m})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-i\int_{\Sigma}{\hbox{d}}^{3}{\bf x}\sqrt{-g}\;\Bigl[\dot{U}_{n}(x)\,U_{m}(x)-U_{n}(x)\,\dot{U}_{m}(x)\Bigr]

(28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{m}-\varepsilon_{n})\int_{\Sigma}{\hbox{d}}^{3}{\bf x}\sqrt{-g}\;U_{n}\,U_{m}=0\,.

It doesn’t matter which
t
we choose for
W
when evaluating these orthogonality conditions provided the falloff of
U_{n}
is sufficiently good at spatial infinity (if this exists), and this is the point of why
W
is defined the way it is. To see why notice
(-\Box+m^{2})U_{n}=0
implies the following chain of equalities

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\int_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}{\hbox{d}}^{4}x\;\sqrt{-g}\Bigl[[(-\Box+m^{2})U_{n}]^{*}U_{m}-U_{n}^{*}[(-\Box+m^{2})U_{m}]\Bigr]

(30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\int_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}{\hbox{d}}^{4}x\;\sqrt{-g}\;\nabla_{\mu}\Bigl[(\nabla^{\mu}U_{n})^{*}U_{m}-U_{n}^{*}(\nabla^{\mu}U_{m})\Bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\oint_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}{\hbox{d}}^{3}x\;\sqrt{-g}\;n_{\mu}\Bigl[(\nabla^{\mu}U_{n})^{*}U_{m}-U_{n}^{*}(\nabla^{\mu}U_{m})\Bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W_{\Sigma}(U_{n},U_{m})-W_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(U_{n},U_{m})\,.

Here the integration in the first line is over a slab of spacetime lying between two constant-
t
slices,
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
. The second line then integrates both terms by parts and the third line uses Gauss’ theorem to write the result in terms of a surface integral over the boundaries of the spacetime region of interest, with
n_{\mu}
being the outward-pointing normal. If there are no spatial boundaries (or if the boundary conditions are chosen at spatial infinity appropriately) then the only boundaries contributing to the integrals are
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Then
n_{\mu}{\hbox{d}}x^{\mu}=\pm{\hbox{d}}t
for the two surfaces, and the last line follows by recognizing that the surface integrals are precisely the Wronskians for each of the bounding constant-
t
surfaces. Comparing first and last lines shows that
W_{\Sigma}(U_{n},U_{m})
does not depend on
\Sigma
.

Given the above conventions and normalization condition, completeness of the modes implies

\sum_{n}u_{n}({\bf x})\,u_{n}^{*}({\bf y})=\frac{\delta^{3}({\bf x},{\bf y})}{[-g({\bf x})]^{1/4}[-g({\bf y})]^{1/4}}\,,

(33)

where the delta function transforms as a bi-density distribution that vanishes when
{\bf x}\neq{\bf y}
and satisfies the defining condition

f({\bf x},t)=\int{\hbox{d}}^{3}{\bf y}\;\delta^{3}({\bf x},{\bf y})\,f({\bf y},t)

(34)

for all
f
without any metrics. The completeness condition is related to the normalization condition because multiplying (
33
) by
\sqrt{-g({\bf y})}\;u_{m}({\bf y})
and integrating over
{\bf y}
must give the tautology
u_{m}({\bf x})=u_{m}({\bf x})
, which it does but only because the
u_{m}
’s are orthogonal and (
26
) implies each mode satisfies the normalization condition

\int_{\Sigma}{\hbox{d}}^{3}{\bf x}\sqrt{-g}\;u_{n}^{*}({\bf x})u_{n}({\bf x})=2\varepsilon_{n}\int_{\Sigma}{\hbox{d}}^{3}{\bf x}\sqrt{-g}\;U_{n}^{*}({\bf x})U_{n}({\bf x})=1\,.

(35)

Finally, the harmonic oscillator (or creation and annihilation) operator algebra is equivalent to the canonical quantization conditions because

[a_{n},a_{m}]=0\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad[a_{n},a_{m}^{\star}]=\delta_{nm}\,,

(36)

imply

\displaystyle[\phi(x),\phi(y)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{nm}\Bigl\{U_{n}(x)\,U^{*}_{m}(y)[a_{n},a^{\star}_{m}]+U^{*}_{n}(x)U_{m}(y)[a_{n}^{\star},a_{m}]\Bigr\}

(38)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{n}}\Bigl\{u_{n}({\bf x})\,u^{*}_{n}({\bf y})-u^{*}_{n}({\bf x})u_{n}({\bf y})\Bigr\}=0\,,

and

\displaystyle[\pi({\bf x},t),\phi({\bf y},t)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\sqrt{-g({\bf x})}\;\sum_{nm}\varepsilon_{n}\Bigl\{U_{n}(x)\,U^{*}_{m}(y)[a_{n},a^{\star}_{m}]-U^{*}_{n}(x)U_{m}(y)[a_{n}^{\star},a_{m}]\Bigr\}

(40)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}\sqrt{-g({\bf x})}\;\sum_{n}\Bigl\{u_{n}({\bf x})\,u^{*}_{n}({\bf y})+u^{*}_{n}({\bf x})u_{n}({\bf y})\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\frac{[-g({\bf x})]^{1/4}}{[-g({\bf y})]^{1/4}}\;\delta^{3}({\bf x},{\bf y})=-i\,\delta^{3}({\bf x},{\bf y})\,.

(41)

Calculation of the energy eigenvalues and eigenstates

1.

The point of the above is that the energy is diagonal when expressed in terms of the eigenstates of
a^{\star}_{n}a_{n}
, as we see by evaluating the Hamiltonian in terms of
a_{n}
and
a_{n}^{\star}
. To this end write

\displaystyle H=\int{\hbox{d}}^{3}{\bf x}\;{\mathcal{}H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\hbox{d}}^{3}{\bf x}\left\{\frac{\pi^{2}}{2\sqrt{-g}}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{-g}\;\Bigl[g^{ij}\,\nabla_{i}\phi\,\nabla_{j}\phi+m^{2}\,\phi^{2}\Bigr]\right\}

(43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\hbox{d}}^{3}{\bf x}\left\{\frac{\pi^{2}}{2\sqrt{-g}}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{-g}\;\phi\Bigl[-g^{ij}\,\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\phi+m^{2}\,\phi\Bigr]\right\}\,,

and show that it can be written

H=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n}\varepsilon_{n}\Bigl(a_{n}^{\star}a_{n}+a_{n}a_{n}^{\star}\Bigr)\,.

(44)

2.

The previous question shows that
H
is diagonal in the basis for which the operators
a_{n}^{\star}a_{n}
are diagonal for all
n
. Show this by using the commutation relation
[a_{n},a_{m}^{\star}]=\delta_{nm}
to rewrite
H
as

H=E_{0}+\sum_{n}\varepsilon_{n}\,a_{n}^{\star}a_{n}\,,

(45)

with the constant
E_{0}
being formally written as

E_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n}\varepsilon_{n}\,.

(46)

This expression is ‘formal’ because the sum typically diverges. It can be regularized in many ways (and you might reasonably wonder whether or not physical results depend on which way is used). One such is zeta-function regularization, which defines

\zeta(s):=\sum_{n}\varepsilon^{-s}\,,

(47)

for complex
s
. This often converges where the real part of
s
is sufficiently large and positive, and one tries to analytically extend this result down to the desired result
E_{0}=\zeta(-1)
. Another way to proceed is instead to differentiate
E_{0}
sufficiently many times with respect to
m^{2}
that the sum converges, and then integrate the sum again to get
E_{0}
,

The energy eigenvalues for
H
are clearly given by
H|\{N_{k}\}\rangle=E|\{N_{k}\}\rangle
with

E=E_{0}+\sum_{n}N_{n}\varepsilon_{n}\,,

(48)

where the next exercise shows the allowed values for the
N_{n}
are
N_{n}=0,1,2,\cdots
. The state
|0\rangle
denotes the ground state (or vacuum) for which
N_{n}=0
for all
n
and has eigenvalue

H|0\rangle=E_{0}|0\rangle\,.

(49)

3.

The basis diagonalizing
a_{n}^{\star}a_{n}
for all
n
is called the ‘occupation-number’ basis and denoted
|\{N_{k}\}\rangle=|N_{n_{1}},N_{n_{2}},N_{n_{3}},\cdots\rangle
where the labels
N_{n}
are the eigenvalues for
a_{n}^{\star}a_{n}
, for all possible values taken by
n
. That is, they satisfy

a_{n}^{\star}a_{n}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle=N_{n}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle\,.

(50)

Prove that the
N_{n}=0,1,2,\cdots
are non-negative integers as follows. First prove
[a^{\star}_{n}a_{n},a_{m}]=-\delta_{nm}a_{n}
and
[a^{\star}_{n}a_{n},a_{m}^{\star}]=+\delta_{nm}a^{\star}_{n}
. Show that these relations imply that if
|\{N_{k}\}\rangle
is an eigenstate with eigenvector of
a_{n}^{\star}a_{n}
with eigenvalue
N_{n}
then
a_{n}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle
is also an eigenstate of
a_{n}^{\star}a_{n}
but with eigenvalue
N_{n}-1
and
a_{n}^{\star}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle
is an eigenvector with eigenvalue
N_{n}+1
.

Next prove
N_{n}\geq 0
by evaluating
\langle\{N_{k}\}|a^{\star}_{n}a_{n}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle=N_{n}\langle\{N_{k}\}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle=N_{n}
and recognizing that the left-hand side is non-negative because it is the norm of the vector
a_{n}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle
. But this is inconsistent with the result that
a_{n}
always lowers the eigenvalue by one unit unless there exists an eigenstate for which
a_{n}|\Psi\rangle=0
. Repeating this argument for all labels
n
shows there must be a state,
|0\rangle
, for which
a_{n}|0\rangle=0
for all
n
, and then all other eigenstates of
a_{n}^{\star}a_{n}
are obtained by acting repeatedly on
|0\rangle
with
a_{n}^{\star}
. (For example consider the particular state
|2_{n_{5}},6_{n_{20}}\rangle
, for which the particle state labeled by
n_{5}
has eigenvalue
N_{n_{5}}=2
for
a_{n_{5}}^{\star}a_{n_{5}}
and the state labeled by
n_{20}
has eigenvalue
N_{n_{20}}=6
for
a_{n_{20}}^{\star}a_{n_{20}}
. This is proportional to
(a_{n_{5}}^{\star})^{2}\;(a_{n_{20}}^{\star})^{6}|0\rangle
, and so on for any other choices for these eigenvalues.)

Path integral method of evaluating the vacuum energy

An alternate way to proceed instead uses the path integral formulation for the effective action

e^{i\Gamma[g]}=\int{\mathcal{}D}\phi\,e^{iS[\phi,g]}\,,

(51)

where the action
S[\phi,g]
is given as the integral over (
16
), regarded as a function of the fields
\phi
and
g_{\mu\nu}
. In this expression
\Gamma[g]
is a contribution to the action for the metric,
g_{\mu\nu}
, obtained after integrating out the field
\phi
. It is to be added to other terms (like the Einstein-Hilbert term), but our interest is in anything of the form
\Gamma=-\int{\hbox{d}}^{4}x\;\sqrt{-g}\;\rho_{v}
, because this gravitates like a cosmological constant (or vacuum energy). For time-translational invariant systems the integral over t diverges proportional to
\int_{-T}^{T}{\hbox{d}}t=T
as
T\to\infty
and it is the energy
E_{0}=-\Gamma/T
that should remain finite in this limit.

Because the functional integral is Gaussian it can be evaluated in terms of a functional determinant of the quadratic operator appearing in the action:
\Delta=(-\Box+m^{2})\delta^{4}(x-y)
.

e^{i\Gamma}=\left[{\det}\Bigl(-\Box+m^{2}-i\epsilon\Bigr)\right]^{-1/2}\,,

(52)

and so

\Gamma=\frac{i}{2}\ln\det\Bigl(-\Box+m^{2}-i\epsilon\Bigr)=\frac{i}{2}\hbox{Tr}\,\ln\Bigl(-\Box+m^{2}-i\epsilon\Bigr)\,.

(53)

Here
\epsilon
is a positive quantity that is taken to zero at the end, and imposes (as usual for a Feynman propagator) the right boundary conditions to describe matrix elements in the vacuum. We suppress the
i\epsilon
in what follows, but recall it when needed by regarding
m^{2}
as having a small negative imaginary part.

To evaluate this again choose eigenfunctions that diagonalize
-g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}
and
i\partial_{t}
. That is choose a basis of functions,
V_{n}(x)
, for which

-g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}V_{n}=\omega_{n}^{2}V_{n}\;\;\hbox{ and }\;\;i\partial_{t}V_{n}=\varepsilon V_{n}\,,

(54)

and so

(-\Box+m^{2})V_{n}=(-\varepsilon^{2}+\omega_{n}^{2}+m^{2})V_{n}

(55)

is diagonalized with eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}=-\varepsilon^{2}+\omega_{n}^{2}+m^{2}=-\varepsilon^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}_{n}
. Notice that unlike the previous section we do not also have
(-\Box+m^{2})U_{n}=0
and so we
cannot
identify
\varepsilon^{2}
with
\varepsilon_{n}^{2}:=\omega_{n}^{2}+m^{2}
. Instead
\varepsilon
is the Fourier transform variable for time, arising generically for time-translationally invariant systems.

In terms of this our operator in this basis is

\langle n\varepsilon|\Delta|r\varepsilon^{\prime}\rangle=(-\varepsilon^{2}+\omega^{2}_{n}+m^{2})\;2\pi\delta(\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{\prime})\delta_{nr}\,,

(56)

and so the trace may be given by taking diagonal elements and summing over their eigenvalues, with

\Gamma(m^{2})=\frac{i}{2}\hbox{Tr}\,\ln\Bigl(-\Box+m^{2}\Bigr)=\frac{i}{2}\sum_{n}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{{\hbox{d}}\varepsilon}{2\pi}\;\ln(- \varepsilon^2 + \omega_n^2 + m^2 )\;2\pi\delta(0)\,.

(57)

The factor of
\delta(0)
arises due to time translation invariance, as may be seen by writing

2\pi\delta(E)=\lim_{T\to\infty}\int_{-T}^{T}{\hbox{d}}t\;e^{-i\varepsilon t}\quad\hbox{and so}\quad 2\pi\delta(0)=\lim_{T\to\infty}T\,,

(58)

and so the well-behaved quantity is the energy

E_{0}=-\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{\Gamma}{T}=-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{n}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{{\hbox{d}}\varepsilon}{2\pi}\;\ln(- \varepsilon^2 + \omega_n^2 + m^2 )\,.

(59)

Again the remaining sums and integrals diverge. The integration over
\varepsilon
passes through singularities at
\pm\varepsilon_{n}
, which we should navigate by Wick rotating. That is, keeping in mind (as usual) that
m^{2}\to m^{2}-i\epsilon
is required for the Feynman propagator, we can rotate our contour of integration counter-clockwise by 90 degrees in the complex
\varepsilon
plane by writing
\varepsilon\to i\,\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle E}
with
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle E}
also running from
-\infty
to
\infty
. The integral converges if we first differentiate with respect to
m^{2}
, so show that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial E_{0}}{\partial m^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{{\hbox{d}}\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle E}}{2\pi}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle E}^{2}+\omega_{n}^{2}+m^{2}}\right)

(61)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{n}\left[\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{n}}\,\arctan\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{n}}\right)\right]^{\infty}_{-\infty}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{n}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{n}}\,,

where, as above,
\varepsilon_{n}=\sqrt{\omega_{n}^{2}+m^{2}}
. Integrating again with respect to
m^{2}
then gives

E_{0}(m^{2})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n}\varepsilon_{n}\,,

(62)

up to an arbitrary
m^{2}
-independent constant. This is the same sum as was obtained in the canonical calculation earlier.

Flat space evaluation

As a particularly simple case consider the case of a flat geometry, for which
-g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}=-\nabla^{2}
can be diagonalized in Fourier space, with eigenfunctions
\exp(i {\bf p} \cdot{\bf x})
and eigenvalues
{\bf p}^{2}
.

In terms of this the required operator in this basis is

\langle p|\Delta|q\rangle=(p_{\mu}p^{\mu}+m^{2})\;(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(p-q)\,,

(63)

and so the trace may be given by taking diagonal elements and summing over their eigenvalues, with

\Gamma(m^{2})=\frac{i}{2}\hbox{Tr}\,\ln\Bigl(-\Box+m^{2}\Bigr)=\frac{i}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{{\hbox{d}}^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\;\ln(p_\mu p^\mu+ m^2 )\;(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(0)\,.

(64)

The additional factor of
\delta^{3}(0)
arises due to spatial translation invariance, as may be seen by writing (as we did before for time)

(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{3}({\bf p})=\lim_{L\to\infty}\int_{-L}^{L}{\hbox{d}}^{3}{\bf x}\;e^{i{\bf p}\cdot{\bf x}}\quad\hbox{and so}\quad(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{3}(0)=\lim_{L\to\infty}L^{3}\,,

(65)

and so is proportional to the volume of space (as well as the previous proportionality to
T
). The well-behaved quantity for infinite translationally invariant systems is therefore the energy
density
,

\rho_{v}=\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{E_{0}}{L^{3}}=-\lim_{L,T\to\infty}\frac{\Gamma}{TL^{3}}=-\frac{i}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{{\hbox{d}}^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\;\ln(p_\mu p^\mu+ m^2 )\,.

(66)

To avoid the singularities at
p^{0}=\pm\sqrt{{\bf p}^{2}+m^{2}}
, we again Wick rotate. In the resulting euclidean integral the angular integrals can be done once and for all, giving a factor of the volume of the unit 3-sphere:
2\pi^{2}
. The remaining integral converges if we first differentiate with respect to
m^{2}
thrice, so show that

\left({\frac{\partial}{\partial m^{2}}}\right)^{3}\rho_{v}=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{p_{\scriptscriptstyle E}^{3}{\hbox{d}}p_{\scriptscriptstyle E}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\;\frac{2}{(p_{\scriptscriptstyle E}^{2}+m^{2})^{3}}=\frac{1}{32\pi^{2}m^{2}}\,,

(67)

and so integrating three times with respect to
m^{2}
then gives

\rho_{v}=\frac{m^{4}}{64\pi^{2}}\ln\left(\frac{m^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)+Am^{4}+Bm^{2}+C\,,

(68)

where
\mu
,
A
,
B
and
C
are arbitrary
m^{2}
-independent constants. Although the values of
A
,
B
and
C
can depend on how the integrals were regulated, the logarithmic term cannot.

SOLUTION
: Calculation of vacuum energy for a scalar field in a static spacetime.

1.

The Hamiltonian can be written as,

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}x\left\{\frac{\pi^{2}}{2\sqrt{-g}}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{-g}\phi\left[-g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}+m^{2}\right]\phi\right\}

(69)

where the canonical field and it’s conjugate momenta may be expanded in terms of annihilation and creation operators,

\displaystyle\phi(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}\left[a_{n}U_{n}(x)+a_{n}^{*}U_{n}^{*}(x)\right]

(70)

\displaystyle\pi(x)=-i\sqrt{-g}\sum_{n}\epsilon_{n}\left[a_{n}U_{n}(x)-a_{n}^{*}U_{n}^{*}(x)\right]

(71)

where the mode functions
U_{n}
are orthogonal and normalized with respect to the Klein-Gordon norm.
This gives,

H=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n}\epsilon_{n}\left(a_{n}^{*}a_{n}+a_{n}a_{n}^{*}\right)

(72)

which shows that number eigenstates (of
a_{n}^{*}a_{n}
) are also energy eigenstates.

2.

Using
\left[a_{n},a_{n}^{*}\right]=\delta_{nm}
, the Hamiltonian can be written,

H=E_{0}+\sum_{n}\epsilon_{n}a_{n}^{*}a_{n},

(73)

where
E_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{n}\epsilon_{n}
is the zero point energy.

3.

Using the canonical commutation relations, one has that,

\displaystyle[a_{n}^{*}a_{n},a_{m}]

\displaystyle=-\delta_{nm}a_{n}

(74)

\displaystyle[a_{n}^{*}a_{n},a_{m}^{*}]

\displaystyle=+\delta_{nm}a_{n}^{*}

(75)

Then for a state with definite occupation numbers,
a_{n}^{*}a_{n}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle=N_{n}|\{N_{n}\}\rangle
, we have that,

\displaystyle a_{m}^{*}a_{m}\left(a_{n}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle\right)=a_{n}a_{m}a_{m}^{*}|\{N_{n}\}\rangle-\delta_{nm}a_{n}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle=(N_{m}-\delta_{nm})a_{n}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle

(76)

and so
a_{n}
lowers the occupation number
N_{n}
by one, and similarly,

\displaystyle a_{m}^{*}a_{m}\left(a_{n}^{*}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle\right)=a_{n}^{*}a_{m}a_{m}^{*}|\{N_{n}\}\rangle+\delta_{nm}a_{n}^{*}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle=(N_{m}+\delta_{nm})a_{n}^{*}|\{N_{k}\}\rangle

(77)

and so
a_{n}^{*}
increases
N_{n}
by one.

In order for the Hilbert space to have positive-definite norm, there must be a null state,
a_{n}|0\rangle=0
, which prevents any
N_{n}
from becoming negative (such states would have negative norm). From this single state, repeated action of
a_{n}^{*}
generates the entire Fock space of possible states.

{exercisenb}

Quantum fluctuations of a scalar field in a class of inflationary spacetimes

For a change of pace we work in the Schrödinger picture, rather than the Heisenberg picture, and so compute the vacuum wavefunctional,
\Psi[\varphi,\,t]
, for a scalar field.

Action and Hamiltonian

Our starting point is the Lagrangian density for a spectator scalar

L=\int{\hbox{d}}^{3}x\,a(t)^{3}\,\left[\frac{1}{2}\,\dot{\phi}^{2}-\frac{1}{2\,a^{2}(t)}\left(\nabla\phi\right)^{2}-\frac{m^{2}(t)}{2}\,\phi^{2}\right]\,,

(78)

in an FRW spacetime with metric

{\hbox{d}}s^{2}\,=\,-{\hbox{d}}t^{2}+a^{2}(t){\hbox{d}}\vec{x}^{2}

(79)

and Hubble parameter
H(t)\,=\,\dot{a}/a
. Here
m(t)
denotes the (possibly slowly time-dependent) mass.

Find the canonical momentum,
\pi_{k}
, for each Fourier mode,
\varphi_{k}
, of the scalar field. Given the quantization condition
\pi_{k}=-i\delta/\delta\varphi_{k}
, show that the Hamiltonian density in Schrödinger representation can be expressed in Fourier space as

{\mathcal{}H}={\mathcal{}H}_{0}+\sum_{k}{\mathcal{}H}_{k}\,,

(80)

with
{\mathcal{}H}_{k}
for
k\neq 0
given by

{\mathcal{}H}_{k}\,=\,-\frac{1}{a^{3}}\,\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta{\varphi}_{k}\,\delta{\varphi}_{-k}}+{a^{3}}\,\left[\frac{c_{s}^{2}\,k^{2}}{a^{2}}+m^{2}\right]\,\varphi_{k}\varphi_{-k}

(81)

where
\varphi^{*}_{k}\,=\,\varphi_{-k}
.

Ground state wave functional

Use this Hamiltonian to evolve the state wave-functional,
\Psi=\prod_{k}\Psi_{k}
, according to the Schrödinger equation,

i\,\frac{\partial\Psi_{k}}{\partial t}\,=\,{\mathcal{}H}_{k}\,\Psi_{k}\,,

(82)

and for free fields seek solutions subject to a Gaussian ansatz,

\Psi[\varphi]=\prod_{k}\Psi_{k}[\varphi]\,=\,\prod_{k}{\mathcal{}N}_{k}(t)\,\exp\Bigl\{-a^{3}(t)\,\Bigl[\,\alpha_{k}(t)\,\varphi_{k}\,\varphi_{-k}\Bigr]\Bigr\}

(83)

and show that the variance of
\varphi_{k}
,
\langle|\varphi_{k}|^{2}\rangle
, is given by

[a^{3}(\alpha_{k}+\alpha_{k}^{*})]^{-1}
. Determine the evolution equations for the functions
{\mathcal{}N}_{k}(t)
,
\alpha_{k}(t)
by substituting into (
82
). Show that they imply
\alpha_{k}
must satisfy

0=\dot{\alpha}_{k}+i\,\alpha_{k}^{2}+3\,H\,\alpha_{k}-i\left(\frac{k^{2}}{a^{2}}+m^{2}\right)\hskip 28.45274pt{\rm for}\,\,k\geq 0

(84)

where all quantities (including the Hubble parameter) can be time dependent, and the dot denotes derivative
with respect to time. The additional equation for
{\mathcal{}N}_{k}
ensures it evolves in a way that is consistent with normalization, but is not needed in what follows.

The solution for
\alpha_{k}
can be made very explicit if we assume power-law expansion,
a=a_{0}(t/t_{0})^{p}
(so that
H=p/t
and
\epsilon=-\dot{H}/H^{2}=1/p
) and a time-independent ratio
m/H
. (Show that de Sitter space can be obtained as the special case where
p\to\infty
and so
\epsilon\to 0
.)

Equations of the form of (
84
) are integrated by changing variables from
\alpha_{k}
to
u_{k}
where

\alpha_{k}=-i\left(\frac{\dot{u}_{k}}{u_{k}}\right)=\-i\,a\,H\,\left[\frac{\partial_{a}\,u_{k}(a)}{u_{k}(a)}\right]\,.

(85)

Show that (
84
) is then satisfied if
u_{k}
solves the Klein-Gordon equation,

\ddot{u}_{k}+3H\,\dot{u}_{k}+\left(\frac{k^{2}}{a^{2}}+m^{2}\right)u_{k}=0\,.

(86)

For constant
\epsilon
and
m^{2}/H^{2}
show that this is solved by

u_{k}(a)=\tilde{\mathcal{}C}_{k}\,y^{q}\,\sigma_{k}(y),

(87)

where
\tilde{\mathcal{}C}_{k}
is
a
-independent, provided
q
and
y
are chosen as

q=\frac{3-\epsilon}{2\,(1-\epsilon)}\,,

(88)

and

y(a,k):=\frac{1}{(1-\epsilon)}\left(\frac{k}{aH}\right)=\frac{1}{(1-\epsilon)}\left(\frac{k}{a_{0}H_{0}}\right)\left(\frac{a_{0}}{a}\right)^{1-\epsilon}\,.

(89)

The point of these changes of variables is that they turn eqn (
86
) into the Bessel equation for
\sigma_{k}
:

y^{2}\,\sigma_{k}^{\prime\prime}+y\,\sigma_{k}^{\prime}+\left(y^{2}-\nu^{2}\right)\,\sigma_{k}=0\,,

(90)

where primes here denote derivatives with respect to
y
. Show that the order
\nu
is given by

\nu^{2}=\frac{1}{(1-\epsilon)^{2}}\left[\frac{(3-\epsilon)^{2}}{4}-\frac{m^{2}}{H^{2}}\right]\,.

(91)

The solutions for
\sigma_{k}
are (naturally) Bessel functions, and demanding agreement with the adiabatic vacuum before horizon exit tells us

u_{k}\propto\exp\left[\mp i\int{\hbox{d}}t\left(\frac{k}{a}\right)\right]\propto e^{\pm iy}\qquad\hbox{for $k/a\gg H$}\,,

(92)

of which we choose the lower sign since this turns out below to ensure the real part of
\alpha_{k}
is positive (as required to ensure
\Psi_{k}
can be normalized). Show that this fixes the mode functions to be

u_{k}(a)=\tilde{\mathcal{}C}_{k}\,y^{q}(a,k)\,H^{(2)}_{\nu}\left[y(a,k)\right]=\frac{{\mathcal{}C}_{k}}{\sqrt{a^{3}H}}\;H^{(2)}_{\nu}\left[y(a,k)\right]

(93)

where
{\mathcal{}C}_{k}\propto k^{q}\tilde{\mathcal{}C}_{k}
relabels the integration constants and
H^{(2)}_{\nu}
is the Hankel function of the second kind. The second equality in (
93
) follows from eqn (
88
), which implies
a^{3}Hy^{2q}
is time-independent. Notice this reduces to the solution for a massive field in de Sitter space in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
.

Although
{\mathcal{}C}_{k}
drops out of (
85
) and (so does not contribute directly to
\alpha_{k}
), some later formulae are simpler if we choose
{\mathcal{}C}_{k}
so that the Wronskian,

{\mathcal{}W}(u,v):=a^{3}(u^{*}\dot{v}-v^{*}\dot{u})\,,

(94)

satisfies
{\mathcal{}W}(u,u)=i
. Prove that in this case is the expression for the real and imaginary parts of
\alpha_{k}
become

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}+\alpha_{k}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\left(\frac{u_{k}^{*}\dot{u}_{k}-u_{k}\dot{u}_{k}^{*}}{|u_{k}|^{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{a^{3}\,|u_{k}|^{2}}

(95)

\displaystyle\hbox{and}\quad\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\,a\,H\,\left[\frac{\partial_{a}\,\left(|u_{k}|^{2}\right)}{|u_{k}|^{2}}\right]\,.

(96)

What does the first of these imply for the variance of
\varphi_{k}
in terms of
u_{k}
?

Because
{\mathcal{}W}
is independent of time (when evaluated with solutions to (
86
) it is convenient to compute the implications for
{\mathcal{}C}_{k}
in the remote past, where
k\gg aH
, in which case the Hankel function has the asymptotic form

H^{(2)}_{\nu}(y)\to\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi y}}\;e^{-iy+\frac{i\pi}{2}\left(\nu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\quad\hbox{for $y\to\infty$}\,.

(97)

Use this to show

\left|{\mathcal{}C}_{k}\right|^{2}=\frac{\pi}{4(1-\epsilon)}\,,

(98)

for all
k
and
\nu
.

Consequently the quantity relevant to fluctuations in the lectures is

|u_{k}|^{2}=\frac{\pi}{4(1-\epsilon)a^{3}H}\,|H_{\nu}^{(2)}(y)|^{2}\,.

(99)

Use the asymptotic expression

H^{(2)}_{\nu}\left(y\right)\to\frac{i\Gamma(\nu)}{\pi}\,\left(\frac{y}{2}\right)^{-\nu}\quad\hbox{for $y\to 0$}\,,

(100)

to derive the small-
k
limit

|u_{k}|^{2}\to\frac{2^{2\nu-2}|\Gamma(\nu)|^{2}(1-\epsilon)^{2\nu-1}}{\pi a^{3}H}\,\left(\frac{aH}{k}\right)^{2\nu}\,.

(101)

Evaluate this for the case
\nu=\frac{3}{2}
(which for the de Sitter case
\epsilon=0
is a massless scalar field) and show that it agrees with the result obtained using the mode function directly, which in the case
\nu=\frac{3}{2}
is very simple:

u_{k}=-(1-\epsilon)\,\frac{H}{\sqrt{2k^{3}}}\;(y-i)e^{-iy}\qquad\hbox{for $\nu=\frac{3}{2}$}

(102)

up to an irrelevant phase. Prove that this does solve the Klein Gordon equation in the case
\nu=\frac{3}{2}
.

The power spectrum
\Delta^{2}(k)
is proportional to
k^{3}|u_{k}|^{2}
evaluated for
k\ll aH
. For de Sitter space
H
is constant, and in this case what is the predicted
k
-dependence for
k^{3}|u_{k}|^{2}
when
\nu=\frac{3}{2}
? When
\epsilon\neq 0

H
is time dependent and we are supposed to evaluate
H
at the moment where
aH=k
. If this were the whole story (and it is not quite), and if
\Delta^{2}(k)\propto A(k/k_{0})^{n_{s}-1}
, what is the prediction for
n_{s}
as a function of
\epsilon
?

SOLUTION

Action and Hamiltonian:

\pi=\frac{\delta S}{\delta\dot{\phi}}=a^{3}\dot{\varphi}

(103)

Naïvely, one would define,

\pi_{k}=-i\omega_{k}a^{3}\varphi_{k}

which is an explicitly time-dependent momentum. Unlike on flat space, where translation invariance guarantees constant momenta, on FLRW we can have time-dependence. The on-shell condition is,

\omega_{k}^{2}=c_{s}^{2}k^{2}+m^{2}

where
c_{s}
is the sound speed for the scalar fluctuations on this background.

The Hamiltonian is given by,

\displaystyle H:=\pi\varphi-\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=a^{3}\left[\frac{1}{2a^{3}}\pi^{2}+\phi\frac{-\nabla^{2}/a^{2}+m^{2}}{2}\phi\right]

(104)

\displaystyle=a^{3}\left[\frac{1}{a^{3}}\pi_{k}\pi_{-k}+\left(\frac{c_{s}^{2}k^{2}}{a^{2}}+m^{2}\right)\varphi_{k}\varphi_{-k}\right]

(105)

Ground state wave functional:

Using the Gaussian ansatz,

\Psi[\varphi]=\prod_{k}\mathcal{N}_{k}(t)\text{exp}\left\{-a^{3}(t)\left[\alpha_{k}(t)\varphi_{k}\varphi_{-k}\right]\right\}

(106)

we can solve the Schrödinger equation,

i\frac{\partial\Psi_{k}}{\partial t}=\mathcal{H}_{k}\Psi_{k}

(107)

to find

i\frac{\dot{\mathcal{N}}_{k}}{\mathcal{N}_{k}}-ia^{3}\left(3H\alpha_{k}+\dot{\alpha}_{k}\right)\varphi_{k}\varphi_{-k}=\alpha_{k}+a^{3}\left(-\alpha_{k}^{2}+\frac{c_{s}^{2}k^{2}}{a^{2}}+m^{2}\right)\varphi_{k}\varphi_{-k}

(108)

As this holds for all
\varphi_{k}
, we must have that,

0=\dot{\alpha_{k}}+i\alpha_{k}^{2}+3H\alpha_{k}-i\left(\frac{k^{2}}{a^{2}}+m^{2}\right)\;\;\;\;\text{for }k\geq 0

(109)

along with the condition that
\dot{\mathcal{N}}_{k}=i\alpha_{k}\mathcal{N}_{k}
.

Power-law solutions:

If
a=a_{0}(t/t_{0})^{p}
, then
H=p/t
. We can take
p\to\infty
in order to set
\dot{H}/H^{2}\to 0
, and simultaneously take the limit
t=t_{0}+\delta t\to t_{0}
so that,

a(t_{0})=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta t\to 0\\
H\to\infty\end{subarray}}\text{exp}\left(H(t_{0}+\delta t)\text{log}(1+\frac{\delta t}{t_{0}})\right)=\begin{cases}\;\;a_{0}\;\;\;\;&\text{ if }H\delta t\to 0,\\
a_{0}\;e^{H_{0}t_{0}}\;\;\;\;&\text{ if }H\delta t\to H_{0}t_{0},\\
\;\;\;0\;\;\;\;&\text{ if }H\delta t\to\infty\end{cases}

(110)

where
H_{0}
is then the constant curvature of the dS space.

Using
\dot{\alpha}_{k}=-i\ddot{u}_{k}/u_{k}-i\alpha_{k}^{2}
, we get the Klein-Gordon equation for
u_{k}
. Then changing variables with
\dot{y}=-(1-\epsilon)Hy
, we find,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{k}(1-\epsilon)^{2}H^{2}y^{q}

\displaystyle\Big\{y^{2}\sigma_{k}^{\prime\prime}+y\sigma_{k}^{\prime}\left(1+2q+\frac{\epsilon-3}{1-\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sigma_{k}\left(y^{2}+\frac{m^{2}}{H^{2}(1-\epsilon)^{2}}+q^{2}+\frac{q(\epsilon-3)}{1-\epsilon}\right)\Big\}=0

(111)

and so choosing
q=(3-\epsilon)/2(1-\epsilon)
we arrive at Bessel’s equation for
\sigma_{k}
, with order

\nu^{2}=\frac{1}{(1-\epsilon)^{2}}\left[\frac{(3-\epsilon)^{2}}{4}-\frac{m^{2}}{H^{2}}\right]

(112)

The Bessel functions of first and second kind have the following asymptotic behavior,

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to\infty}J_{n}(z)

\displaystyle\sim\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi z}}\,\text{cos}\left(z-\frac{m\pi}{2}-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)

(113)

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to\infty}Y_{n}(z)

\displaystyle\sim\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi z}}\,\text{sin}\left(z-\frac{m\pi}{2}-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)

(114)

and therefore the desired asymptotic behavior for
\sigma
is given by a Hankel functions of the second kind,

H^{(2)}_{n}(z)=J_{n}(z)-iY_{n}(z)

This gives,

u_{k}=\mathcal{C}_{k}/\sqrt{a^{3}H}\,H_{\nu}^{(2)}(\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\frac{k}{aH})

The Wronskian,

\mathcal{W}(u,u)=a^{3}\left[u^{*}\dot{u}-u\dot{u}^{*}\right]=i

(115)

The corresponding
\alpha_{k}
are,

\alpha_{k}=-i\frac{\dot{u}_{k}}{u_{k}}\;\;\implies\;\;\alpha_{k}+\alpha_{k}^{*}=-i\frac{\dot{u}_{k}u_{k}^{*}-\dot{u}_{k}^{*}u_{k}}{|u_{k}|^{2}}=\frac{1}{a^{3}|u_{k}|^{2}}

\alpha_{k}=-iaH\frac{\partial_{a}u_{k}}{u_{k}}\;\;\implies\;\;\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{*}=-iaH\frac{\partial_{a}u_{k}u_{k}^{*}+\partial_{a}u_{k}^{*}u_{k}}{|u_{k}|^{2}}

The variance of
\varphi_{k}
is,

\langle|\varphi_{k}|^{2}\rangle=\frac{1}{a^{3}(\alpha_{k}+\alpha_{k}^{*})}=|u_{k}|^{2}

To find
\mathcal{C}_{k}
, we use the asymptotics of the Hankel function to write,

u_{k}\sim\frac{\mathcal{C}_{k}}{\sqrt{a^{3}H}}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi y}}e^{-iy+\tfrac{i\pi}{2}(\nu+\tfrac{1}{2})}

(116)

\mathcal{W}(u,u)=a^{3}\left(\frac{|\mathcal{C}_{k}|^{2}}{a^{3}H}\frac{2}{\pi y}\;2i(1-\epsilon)Hy\right)=i

(117)

\implies\;\;\;\;|\mathcal{C}_{k}|^{2}=\frac{\pi}{4(1-\epsilon)}

The variance is then given by,

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to 0}|u_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\to\frac{\pi}{4(1-\epsilon)a^{3}H}\left|\frac{i\Gamma(\nu)}{\pi}\left(\frac{y}{2}\right)^{-\nu}\right|^{2}

(118)

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{2\nu-2}|\Gamma(\nu)|^{2}(1-\epsilon)^{2\nu-1}}{\pi a^{3}H}\left(\frac{aH}{k}\right)^{2\nu}

(119)

Massless modes:

In the de Sitter limit,
\epsilon=0
, we find that,

\nu^{2}=\frac{9}{4}-\frac{m^{2}}{H^{2}}

and so massless fields on de Sitter correspond to
\nu=3/2
.

Specializing to
\nu=3/2
, and using
\Gamma(3/2)=\sqrt{\pi}/2
,

|u_{k}|^{2}\to\frac{(1-\epsilon)^{2}H^{2}}{2k^{3}}

(120)

Note that this agrees with the exact mode function,

u_{k}=-(1-\epsilon)\frac{H}{\sqrt{2k^{3}}}(y-i)e^{-iy}\;\;\implies\;\;|u_{k}|^{2}=|1-\epsilon|^{2}\frac{H}{2k^{3}}|1+iy|^{2}

This corresponds to a
\sigma_{k}
,

\sigma_{k}=-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}y^{-3/2}(y-i)e^{-iy}

(121)

\implies\;\;y^{2}\sigma^{\prime\prime}_{k}+y\sigma_{k}^{\prime}=-(y^{2}-\frac{3}{2})\sigma_{k}

and therefore
\sigma_{k}
satisfies the Bessel equation with
\nu=3/2
.

Considering,

k^{3}|u_{k}|^{2}\Big|_{\nu=3/2}\to\frac{1}{2}(1-\epsilon)^{2}H^{2}\;\;\;\;\text{for}\;\;k\ll aH

we conclude that
k^{3}|u_{k}|^{2}
is
k
-independent on de Sitter,

k^{3}|u_{k}|^{2}\Big|_{\nu=3/2}\to\frac{1}{2}H_{0}^{2}

On FLRW, if we treat
\epsilon
as a constant then we can solve,

a(t)=k_{0}\left(\epsilon(t-t_{0})\right)^{1/\epsilon}

aH=k\;\;\implies\;\;H=(k/k_{0})^{-\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}}

and so we find that,

n_{s}-1=\frac{-2\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}(\dot{\epsilon})

(122)

This is the effect that a spectator field has on the spectral tilt—note that there must also be a inflation field to drive the expansion.

6
EFT of Large-Scale Structure (Baldauf)

The series of four lectures
[
3
]
provide an introduction to the topic of
Large-Scale Structure (LSS) with an emphasis on the EFT approach. The lecture starts with
a general introduction to LSS phenomenology and relevant statistical tools.
Then, the standard cosmological perturbation theory, which allows to compute relevant observables,
is developed. Building on shortcomings of standard perturbation theory, the EFT approach
is introduced with emphasis on different problems it successfully addresses.
Eventually, relation to observations of biased tracers is discussed together with
the need to account for redshift-space distortion.

This section contains five introductory problems useful for a familiarization with the different
aspects of LSS theory. The first two problems deal with standard phenomenology in cosmology as well as
some statistical aspects of Gaussian fields. Problems three and four cover fluid mechanic aspects
in an expanding Universe and problem five is an application of perturbation theory.
Particular aspects of EFT are briefly touched via questions about counterterms and
UV-sensitivity in problem one and five.
Equations from the lecture notes are referred to with a prefix L, e.g. eqn (L1.1).

Exercise 6.1
.

Clustering of Fixed Height Subsamples
Consider a Gaussian random
field
\delta
described by a power
spectrum
P
. The corresponding real space correlation
function is
\xi
and the variance
\sigma^{2}=\xi(0)
. Consider
the PDF of fluctuations

\mathbb{P}_{\text{2pt}}(\delta_{1},\delta_{2}|r)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}|C(r)|}\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}Y^{T}C^{-1}(r)Y\right]

(1)

where

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{1}\\
\delta_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\sigma\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{1}\\
\nu_{2}\end{pmatrix}

(2a)

\displaystyle C_{ij}(r)

\displaystyle=\langle\delta_{i}\delta_{j}\rangle=\sigma^{2}\delta^{(K)}+(1-\delta^{(K)})\xi(r)

(2b)

are respectively the state vector and the covariance matrix.
The correlation matrix of the field can be recovered as

\xi(r)=\langle\delta(\textbf{x})\delta(\textbf{x}+\textbf{r})\rangle=\sigma^{4}\int\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}\int\mathrm{d}\nu_{2}\ \nu_{1}\nu_{2}\ \mathbb{P}_{\text{2pt}}(\sigma\nu_{1},\sigma\nu_{2}|r)

(3)

Consider the subset of fluctuations of fixed amplitude

\nu_{c}
and calculate their correlation function

\xi_{c}(r)=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\text{2pt}}(\sigma\nu_{1},\sigma\nu_{2}|r)}{\mathbb{P}_{\text{1pt}}(\sigma\nu_{c})\mathbb{P}_{\text{1pt}}(\sigma\nu_{c})}-1

(4)

For large separations this allows an expansion in the small
quantity
\xi/\sigma^{2}
. Write down this expansion to
second order. The prefactors of this expansion are
called bias parameters. Fourier transform the expression
to k-space and consider the low-k limit.
Can you identify contributions that would require a counterterm?

Remark:
The above model can be used to model regions
that will eventually form dark matter halos, i.e.,
formation sites of galaxies. In this context the field is smoothed
on a scale
R
to consider fluctuations of a
given mass
M\propto\overline{\rho}R^{3}

\delta_{R}(\textbf{x})=\int d^{3}xW_{R}(|\textbf{x}-\textbf{x}^{\prime}|)\delta(\textbf{x}^{\prime})~.

(5)

where
W_{R}(r)
is a Gaussian or top hat filter.

SOLUTION:

Computation of
\xi_{c}(r)

As
\delta
is a Gaussian random field of variance
\xi(\textbf{r})
,

\delta_{1}=\delta(\textbf{x})
and
\delta_{2}=\delta(\textbf{x}+\textbf{r})
are,
by definition, Gaussian random variables of variance
\xi(0)=\sigma^{2}
.
Thus,

\mathbb{P}_{\text{1pt}}(\sigma\nu_{1})=\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left[-\frac{\nu_{1}^{2}}{2}\right]\ .

(6)

Moreover,

C(r)=\left(\begin{matrix}\sigma^{2}&\xi(r)\\
\xi(r)&\sigma^{2}\end{matrix}\right)

(7)

so that,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{C(r)}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{4}-\xi(r)^{2}

(8a)

\displaystyle C^{-1}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{C(r)}}\left(\begin{matrix}\sigma^{2}&-\xi(r)\\
-\xi(r)&\sigma^{2}\end{matrix}\right)\ .

(8b)

Using (
8
) to compute
\mathbb{P}_{\text{2pt}}
and plugging it into (
4
)
(together with the explicit form of
\mathbb{P}_{\text{1pt}}
,) one gets

\xi_{c}(r)=\sqrt{\frac{1}{1-\left(\frac{\xi(r)}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{2}}}\exp\left[\nu_{c}^{2}\frac{\frac{\xi(r)}{\sigma^{2}}}{1+\frac{\xi(r)}{\sigma^{2}}}\right]-1\ .

(9)

In the following, the explicit
r
dependence in
\xi
is dropped off.

Expansion in
\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}

Let us assume
\xi\ll\sigma^{2}
. Expanding in this case,

\displaystyle\xi_{c}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\left[\nu_{c}^{2}\frac{\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}}{1+\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}}\right]-1

(10)

\displaystyle=\left\{1+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{2}+O\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{3}\right\}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\times\exp\left[\nu_{c}^{2}\left\{\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}-\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{2}+O\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{3}\right\}\right]-1

(11)

\displaystyle=\left\{1+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{2}+O\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{3}\right\}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\times\left\{1+\nu_{c}^{2}\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}+\left(\nu_{c}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\nu_{c}^{4}\right)\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{2}+O\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{3}\right\}-1

(12)

and finally,

\xi_{c}=\nu_{c}^{2}\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}+\left(\frac{1}{2}+\nu_{c}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\nu_{c}^{4}\right)\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{2}+O\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{3}\ .

(13)

Low-
k
limit

As
P(\textbf{k})=\text{FT}[\xi](\textbf{k})
, the Fourier transformed of (
13
) reads

P_{c}(\textbf{k})=\nu_{c}^{2}\frac{P(\textbf{k})}{\sigma^{2}}+\left(\frac{1}{2}+\nu_{c}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\nu_{c}^{4}\right)\frac{\left(P*P\right)(\textbf{k})}{\sigma^{4}}\ .

(14)

where
P_{c}
is the Fourier transformed of
\xi_{c}
.
Taking the low-
k
limit, we have

P(\textbf{k})\propto k^{n_{S}}

(15)

and

\displaystyle(P*P)(\textbf{k})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}}P(\textbf{q})P(\textbf{k}-\textbf{q})

(16)

\displaystyle\simeq\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}}P(\textbf{q})P(-\textbf{q})

(17)

\displaystyle\propto k^{0}\ .

(18)

Therefore, considering
n_{S}>0
, the first term in (
14
) goes to
0
in the low-
k
limit, while the second term goes to a constant, provided that (
17
) is converging to a non-zero constant.

The necessity (or not) of a counterterm is analyzed by looking at the UV sensitivity of each term.
Consider a certain regularization/smoothing of the
\delta
field. The power spectrum
P=P_{R}
picks up
a dependence on the smoothing scale
R
which, when integrated in a UV divergent integral,
creates a dependence of
P_{c}
on
R
.
Thus the
P*P
term will require a counterterm to cancel this dependence (while the
P
term does not)
2
2

2

Even if
the integral would be convergent, the fact that it runs over non-perturbative wavenumber requires
potential counterterms to capture effectively the physics out of the domain of validity, say for
k>k_{\text{NL}}
.
.
More explicitly, let us consider a family of regulator
W_{R}(\textbf{x})
such that
\lim_{R\to 0}W_{R}(\textbf{x})=\delta^{(D)}(\textbf{x})
or equivalently

\lim_{R\to 0}W_{R}(\textbf{q})=1
.
By definition the regularized fluctuation field is,

\delta_{R}(\textbf{x})=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{y}\ W_{R}(\textbf{x}-\textbf{y})\delta(\textbf{y})\ .

(19)

Thus

\displaystyle\xi_{R}(r)

\displaystyle=\left<\delta_{R}(\textbf{x})\delta_{R}(\textbf{x}+\textbf{r})\right>

(20)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{y}\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{y'}\ W_{R}(\textbf{x}-\textbf{y})W_{R}(\textbf{x}+\textbf{r}-\textbf{y'})\left<\delta(\textbf{y})\delta(\textbf{y'})\right>

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{y}\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{r'}\ W_{R}(\textbf{x}-\textbf{y})W_{R}(\textbf{x}-\textbf{y}+\textbf{r}-\textbf{r'})\left<\delta(\textbf{y})\delta(\textbf{y}+\textbf{r'})\right>

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{y}\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{r'}\ W_{R}(\textbf{x}-\textbf{y})W_{R}(\textbf{x}-\textbf{y}+\textbf{r}-\textbf{r'})\xi(\textbf{r'})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{y}\ W_{R}(\textbf{x}-\textbf{y})\left(W_{R}*\xi\right)(\textbf{x}-\textbf{y}+\textbf{r})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{y}\ W_{R}(\textbf{y})\left(W_{R}*\xi\right)(\textbf{y}+\textbf{r})

\displaystyle=\left(\tilde{W}_{R}*W_{R}*\xi\right)(\textbf{r})

(21)

where for the last line we defined

\tilde{W}_{R}(\textbf{x})\equiv W_{R}(-\textbf{x})\ .

(22)

Assuming
W_{R}(\textbf{x})
to be real and even, the Fourier transformed of (
21
) reads

P_{R}(\textbf{q})=W_{R}^{2}(\textbf{q})P(\textbf{q})\ .

(23)

Now getting back to (
14
), the first term proportional to
P_{R}
converges to
P

so that the
R
dependence vanishes (regardless of any assumption on
P
). However in the second term,
one cannot simply take the
R\to 0
limit inside the integral

P_{R}*P_{R}(\textbf{k})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}}W_{R}^{4}(\textbf{q})P(\textbf{q})P(\textbf{k}-\textbf{q})

(24)

as it diverges when assuming boldly the low-
k
form of
P
to hold for arbitrarily high momentum
q
3
3

3

The
conclusion is the same even when taking into account the transfer function
T(\textbf{q})
.
.
Therefore, either we have to take into account the full
k
dependence
of
P
(but its UV part is not known) or we can employ an EFT approach.
In the latter case one assumes the decoupling between the UV physics and checks it a posteriori
(unless it can be proven from a known underlying theory.) Such decoupling has for consequence that
observables are independent of the UV part of
P
. As such, one can use an arbitrary regularized
P_{R}

to the price of adding counterterms in the theory. The
R
dependence of these counterterms are such
that they cancel the arbitrariness of the regularization and allow to recover the independence of observables
regarding the UV physics. In particular, they will cancel the divergence in
P_{R}*P_{R}
when taking

R\to 0
. Eventually, the remaining
R
-independent terms can be matched to experimental data
at a given
k
momentum. In this case the counterterm should have a
k^{0}
dependence.
Such counterterm corresponds to a stochasticity correction
\epsilon_{2,R}
in the power spectrum
of fixed amplitude fluctuations such that

\lim_{R\to 0}\epsilon_{2,R}+\left(\frac{1}{2}+\nu_{c}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\nu_{c}^{4}\right)\frac{\left(P_{R}*P_{R}\right)(\textbf{k})}{\sigma^{4}}

(25)

is finite and denoted as

\epsilon_{2}+\left(\frac{1}{2}+\nu_{c}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\nu_{c}^{4}\right)\frac{\left(P*P\right)(\textbf{k})}{\sigma^{4}}\ .

(26)

Consequently, the model is modified by including the stochasticicy counterterm and
the power spectrum of fixed amplitude fluctuations now reads as

P_{c}(\textbf{k})=\epsilon_{2}+\nu_{c}^{2}\frac{P(\textbf{k})}{\sigma^{2}}+\left(\frac{1}{2}+\nu_{c}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\nu_{c}^{4}\right)\frac{\left(P*P\right)(\textbf{k})}{\sigma^{4}}\ .

(27)

{exercisenb}

Equality Scale
Integrating the Bose-Einstein distribution,
we get that the radiation energy density
is related to the temperature
T_{CMB}
of the CMB photons by

\rho_{rad}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{15}T_{CMB}^{4}
. Use the measured
values of the CMB temperature of
T_{CMB}=2.725
K and matter density

\Omega_{m,0}=0.28
to calculate the scale
factor of matter-radiation equality
a_{eq}
.
Calculate the size of the horizon at
a_{eq}
and the wavenumber

k_{eq}

of fluctuations entering at matter-radiation equality.
This is the characteristic scale, at which the transfer
function transitions from the large scale
k_{0}
behavior
to the small scale
\ln(k)/k^{2}
behavior

SOLUTION:

Scale factor
a_{\text{eq}}
at matter-radiation equality

At matter-radiation equality one has,

\displaystyle\Omega_{r}(a_{\text{eq}})

\displaystyle=\Omega_{m}(a_{\text{eq}})

(28)

\displaystyle\Omega_{r,0}a_{\text{eq}}^{-4}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{m,0}a_{\text{eq}}^{-3}

(29)

\displaystyle a_{\text{eq}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{r,0}}{\Omega_{m,0}}

(30)

\displaystyle a_{\text{eq}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho_{r,0}}{\rho_{c}\Omega_{m,0}}

(31)

Assuming only photons contribute to radiation energy-density, one gets

\rho_{r,0}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{15}T_{\text{CMB}}^{4}
and

\displaystyle a_{\text{eq}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{15}\frac{T_{\text{CMB}}^{4}}{\rho_{c}\Omega_{m,0}}

(33)

\displaystyle a_{\text{eq}}

\displaystyle=8.8\times 10^{-5}h^{-2}\ .

(34)

Contribution of Neutrinos

To be more precise in the computation of
a_{\text{eq}}
, we should take neutrinos into account.
Before
a_{\text{eq}}
, the temperature is sufficiently large so that we can neglect
the contribution from their masses. As for photons, neutrino’s energy density is obtained
by integrating its distribution function (Fermi-Dirac this time)

\rho_{\nu}=2\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\sqrt{\textbf{p}^{2}+m_{\nu}^{2}}}{e^{\frac{p}{T_{\nu}}}+1}\ .

(35)

To see why one can disregard its mass, one can make the change of variable
x=\frac{p}{T_{\nu}}

\rho_{\nu}=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}T_{\nu}^{4}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}x\ x^{2}\frac{\sqrt{x^{2}+\left(\frac{m_{\nu}}{T_{\nu}}\right)^{2}}}{e^{x}+1}

(36)

so that for
T_{\nu}\gg m_{\nu}

\rho_{\nu}=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}T_{\nu}^{4}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}x\ \frac{x^{3}}{e^{x}+1}\ .

(37)

After integration, taking into account 3 families of neutrinos leads to

\rho_{\nu}=3\ \frac{7}{8}\ \frac{\pi^{2}}{15}\ T_{\nu}^{4}\ .

(38)

To get the energy density contribution of neutrinos, we still need to know their temperature
T_{\nu}
.
It can be related to the temperature of photons
T_{\gamma}
from entropy conservation before and after electrons and
positrons annihilate. This is a standard result in cosmology which gives
T_{\nu}=\left(\frac{4}{11}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}T_{\gamma}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\rho_{\nu}

\displaystyle=3\ \frac{7}{8}\left(\frac{4}{11}\right)^{\frac{4}{3}}\ \rho_{\gamma}

(39)

\displaystyle\rho_{r}

\displaystyle=\left(1+3\ \frac{7}{8}\left(\frac{4}{11}\right)^{\frac{4}{3}}\right)\rho_{r}^{\not{\nu}}

(40)

and the correction to
a_{\text{eq}}
reads as

\displaystyle a_{\text{eq}}

\displaystyle=\left(1+3\ \frac{7}{8}\left(\frac{4}{11}\right)^{\frac{4}{3}}\right)a_{\text{eq}}^{\not{\nu}}

(41)

\displaystyle a_{\text{eq}}

\displaystyle=1.5\times 10^{-4}h^{-2}\ .

(42)

Horizon
\chi(a_{\text{eq}})

The size of the horizon at
a_{\text{eq}}
is

\displaystyle\chi(a_{\text{eq}})

\displaystyle=c\int_{0}^{t_{\text{eq}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{a(t)}

(43)

\displaystyle=c\int_{0}^{a_{\text{eq}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}a}{a^{2}H(a)}\ .

(44)

In radiative dominated era,

H(a)\simeq H_{0}\sqrt{\Omega_{r,0}a^{-4}}

(45)

so that,

\displaystyle\chi(a_{\text{eq}})

\displaystyle=\frac{c\ a_{\text{eq}}}{H_{0}\sqrt{\Omega_{r,0}}}

(46)

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{H_{0}\Omega_{m,0}}\sqrt{\Omega_{r,0}}

(47)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1+3\ \frac{7}{8}\left(\frac{4}{11}\right)^{\frac{4}{3}}}\frac{c}{H_{0}\Omega_{m,0}}\frac{\pi\ T_{\text{CMB}}^{2}}{\sqrt{15\ \rho_{c}}}

(48)

\displaystyle\chi(a_{\text{eq}})

\displaystyle=5.1\times 10^{2}h^{-2}\text{Mpc}

(49)

and the corresponding wavenumber
k_{\text{eq}}
gives

\displaystyle k_{\text{eq}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{\chi(a_{\text{eq}})}

(50)

\displaystyle k_{\text{eq}}

\displaystyle=1.2\times 10^{-2}h^{2}\text{Mpc}^{-1}\ .

(51)

Influence of Matter around
a_{\text{eq}}

The approximation made in (
45
) might be too crude, especially near
matter-radiation equality. To be more precise, let us do the calculation taking into account
matter energy density. Thus (
45
) becomes

H(a)\simeq H_{0}\sqrt{\Omega_{r,0}a^{-4}+\Omega_{m,0}a^{-3}}

(52)

so that

\displaystyle\chi(a_{\text{eq}})

\displaystyle=c\int_{0}^{a_{\text{eq}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}a}{a^{2}H(a)}

(53)

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{H_{0}\sqrt{\Omega_{r,0}}}\int_{0}^{a_{\text{eq}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}a}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\Omega_{m,0}}{\Omega_{r,0}}a}}

(54)

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{H_{0}\sqrt{\Omega_{r,0}}}\int_{0}^{a_{\text{eq}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}a}{\sqrt{1+\frac{a}{a_{\text{eq}}}}}

(55)

\displaystyle=\frac{c\ a_{\text{eq}}}{H_{0}\sqrt{\Omega_{r,0}}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\sqrt{1+x}}

(56)

\displaystyle=\frac{c\ a_{\text{eq}}}{H_{0}\sqrt{\Omega_{r,0}}}\left(2\sqrt{2}-2\right)

(57)

\displaystyle\chi(a_{\text{eq}})

\displaystyle=4.2\times 10^{2}h^{-2}\text{Mpc}

(58)

and the wavenumber at equivalence becomes

k_{\text{eq}}=1.4\times 10^{-2}h^{2}\text{Mpc}^{-1}\ .

(59)

{exercisenb}

Fluid Equations

Using the definitions of density, mean streaming
velocity and velocity dispersion in
terms of the distribution function
f(\textbf{x},\textbf{p},\tau)

and the conservation of phase space density
df/d\tau=0
, derive
the continuity and Euler equations for collisionless dark matter.
You will need to use the energy momentum
conservation of the homogeneous background Universe
\overline{\rho}^{\prime}+3{\mathcal{}H}\overline{\rho}=0
.

SOLUTION:

Vlasov’s equation

Using Liouville’s theorem for the dark matter distribution function
f(\textbf{x},\textbf{p},\tau)
,

\frac{\mathrm{d}f}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}+\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}+\frac{\mathrm{d}p_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\frac{\partial f}{\partial p_{i}}=0\ ,

(60)

where summation on repeated indices are implied. Besides, the equations of motion for collisionless dark matter reads

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{i}}{am}

(61a)

\displaystyle p^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=-am\nabla_{i}\phi

(61b)

with
\phi
the peculiar potential. Hence the collisionless Boltzmann’s equation (or Vlasov’s equation)

\underbrace{\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}+\frac{p_{i}}{am}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}-am\nabla_{i}\phi\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{i}}\right]}_{\mathcal{L}}f=0

(62)

where we have defined the Liouville operator
\mathcal{L}
for notation convenience.

Continuity equation

Now taking the
0^{\text{th}}
order comoving velocity moment of (
62
)

\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{p}\ \mathcal{L}f=0

(63)

one gets,

\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\left(\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{p}f\right)+\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{p}\left(\frac{p_{i}}{am}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}\right)-\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{p}\left(am\nabla_{i}\phi\frac{\partial f}{\partial p_{i}}\right)=0\ .

(64)

Using vanishing boundary conditions on
f
one can discards the
3^{\text{rd}}
term and one obtains,
using definitions of
\rho
and
v_{i}
,

\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\left(\frac{a^{3}}{m}\rho\right)+\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\left(v_{i}\frac{a^{3}}{m}\rho\right)=0\ .

(65)

Developing
\rho=\bar{\rho}(1+\delta)
and using the energy-momentum conservation of the
homogeneous background universe, i.e.
\bar{\rho}^{\prime}+3\mathcal{H}\bar{\rho}=0
,

\displaystyle\frac{a^{3}}{m}\bar{\rho}^{\prime}(1+\delta)+\frac{a^{3}}{m}\bar{\rho}\delta^{\prime}+3\frac{a^{2}a^{\prime}}{m}\bar{\rho}(1+\delta)+\bar{\rho}\frac{a^{3}}{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\left(v_{i}(1+\delta)\right)

\displaystyle=0

(66)

\displaystyle\frac{a^{3}}{m}\bar{\rho}\left\{-3\mathcal{H}+3\mathcal{H}+\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\left(v_{i}(1+\delta)\right)+\delta^{\prime}+3\mathcal{H}\delta-3\mathcal{H}\delta\right\}

\displaystyle=0

(67)

and finally we recover the continuity equation

\delta^{\prime}+\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\left(\left(1+\delta\right)v_{i}\right)=0\ .

(68)

Euler equation

Similarly, for the Euler equation, one needs to take the
1^{\text{st}}
comoving velocity moments

\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{p}\ \frac{p_{i}}{am}\mathcal{L}f=0

(69)

which reads

\left(\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{p}\ \frac{p_{i}}{am}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}\right)+\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{p}\left(\ \frac{p_{i}}{am}\frac{p_{j}}{am}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{j}}\right)-\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{p}\left(\frac{p_{i}}{am}am\nabla_{j}\phi\frac{\partial f}{\partial p_{j}}\right)=0\ .

(70)

Integrating by part, the
1^{\text{st}}
term of (
70
) reads as

\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\left(\frac{a^{3}}{m}\rho v_{i}\right)+\frac{a^{3}\rho}{m}\mathcal{H}v_{i}

(71)

while the
2^{\text{nd}}
term reads as

\frac{a^{3}}{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\left(\sigma_{ij}\rho\right)+\frac{a^{3}}{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\left(v_{i}v_{j}\rho\right)

(72)

and the last term as

-\nabla_{j}\phi\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{p}\ \left[\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{j}}\left(p_{i}f\right)-\frac{\partial p_{i}}{\partial p_{j}}f\right]=\nabla_{j}(\phi)\frac{a^{3}}{m}\rho\ ,

(73)

again assuming vanishing boundary conditions.
Dividing (
70
) by
\frac{a^{3}}{m}\rho
one gets

\frac{1}{a^{3}\rho}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\left(a^{3}\rho v_{i}\right)+\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\left(v_{i}v_{j}\rho\right)+\mathcal{H}v_{i}=-\nabla_{i}(\phi)-\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\left(\sigma_{ij}\rho\right)\ .

(74)

Developing the first two terms and canceling the background contributions using again
\bar{\rho}^{\prime}+3\mathcal{H}\bar{\rho}=0
,
one gets

\frac{1}{a^{3}\rho}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\left(a^{3}\rho v_{i}\right)+\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\left(v_{i}v_{j}\rho\right)\\
=\frac{\partial v_{i}}{\partial\tau}+\frac{v_{i}}{1+\delta}\left[\delta^{\prime}+\frac{\partial\delta}{\partial x_{j}}v_{j}\right]+v_{i}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}v_{j}\right)+\left(v_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)v_{i}\ .

(75)

Eventually, using the continuity relation (
68
) which reads

\delta^{\prime}+\frac{\partial\delta}{\partial x_{j}}v_{j}=-\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}v_{j}\right)\times(1+\delta)

(76)

one gets the Euler equation

\frac{\partial v_{i}}{\partial\tau}+\left(v_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)v_{i}+\mathcal{H}v_{i}=-\nabla_{i}(\phi)-\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\left(\sigma_{ij}\rho\right)\ .

(77)

{exercisenb}

Recursion Relations

Starting from the
k
-space version of the continuity and
Euler equations in a
matter-only EdS Universe, and the ansatz

\delta(\text{\boldmath$k$},\tau)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a^{i}(\tau)\delta^{(i)}(\text{\boldmath$k$})~~~~~~~~~~~,~~~~~~~~~~~\theta(\text{\boldmath$k$},\tau)=-{\mathcal{}H}(\tau)\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a^{i}(\tau)\theta^{(i)}(\text{\boldmath$k$})

derive the recursion relations for the gravitational coupling
kernels
F_{n}
and
G_{n}
relating
the
n
-th order fields
to the linear density fields

\displaystyle\delta^{(n)}(\text{\boldmath$k$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{m=1}^{n}\left\{\int\frac{d^{3}q_{m}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\delta^{(1)}(\text{\boldmath$q$}_{m})\right\}F_{n}(\text{\boldmath$q$}_{1},...,\text{\boldmath$q$}_{n})\delta^{(D)}(\text{\boldmath$k$}-\text{\boldmath$q$}|_{1}^{n})

\displaystyle\theta^{(n)}(\text{\boldmath$k$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{m=1}^{n}\left\{\int\frac{d^{3}q_{m}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\delta^{(1)}(\text{\boldmath$q$}_{m})\right\}G_{n}(\text{\boldmath$q$}_{1},...,\text{\boldmath$q$}_{n})\delta^{(D)}(\text{\boldmath$k$}-\text{\boldmath$q$}|_{1}^{n})

SOLUTION:

Let us consider a matter-only EdS Universe, neglecting vorticity, the decaying mode and taking the ansatz

\displaystyle\delta(\textbf{k},\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a^{n}(\tau)\delta^{(n)}(\textbf{k})

(78a)

\displaystyle\theta(\textbf{k},\tau)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{H}(\tau)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a^{n}(\tau)\theta^{(n)}(\textbf{k})

(78b)

as solution of Euler and continuity equations

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}(\textbf{k})+\theta(\textbf{k})

\displaystyle=-\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}}{2\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q'}}{2\pi}\delta^{(D)}(\textbf{k}-\textbf{q}-\textbf{q'})

(79a)

\displaystyle\phantom{=-\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}}{2\pi}}\times\alpha(\textbf{q},\textbf{q'})\theta(\textbf{q})\delta(\textbf{q'})

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime}(\textbf{k})+\mathcal{H}\theta(\textbf{k})+\frac{3}{2}\Omega_{m}\mathcal{H}\delta(\textbf{k})

\displaystyle=-\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}}{2\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q'}}{2\pi}\delta^{(D)}(\textbf{k}-\textbf{q}-\textbf{q'})

\displaystyle\phantom{=-\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}}{2\pi}}\times\beta(\textbf{q},\textbf{q'})\theta(\textbf{q})\theta(\textbf{q'})\ .

(79b)

In this case
\Omega_{m}=1
and Friedman’s equation gives us

\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(\tau)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\tau)\ .

(80)

Therefore,

\delta^{\prime}(\textbf{k},\tau)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a^{n}(\tau)\left[n\mathcal{H}(\tau)\delta^{(n)}(\textbf{k})\right]

(81a)

and

\theta^{\prime}(\textbf{k},\tau)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a^{n}(\tau)\left[\left(\frac{1}{2}-n\right)\mathcal{H}^{2}(\tau)\theta^{(n)}(\textbf{k})\right]\ .

(81b)

Since we are only interested in the finite perturbation expansion
4
4

4

Note that it is not necessary the case but one could extend the derivations for a given
resummation scheme. For example, using the usual limit of partial sums or
a Borel resummation would lead to similar results as they are both compatible with
product, sum and derivative of series.
The only care to be taken in these cases would be to consider the domain of convergence.
,

\theta\times\delta
at order
n
is just the Cauchy product i.e.

\displaystyle\theta(\textbf{q},\tau)\delta(\textbf{q'},\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a^{n}(\tau)\left[-\mathcal{H}(\tau)\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\theta^{(p)}(\textbf{q})\delta^{(n-p)}(\textbf{q'})\right]

(81c)

\displaystyle\theta(\textbf{q},\tau)\theta(\textbf{q'},\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a^{n}(\tau)\left[\mathcal{H}^{2}(\tau)\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\theta^{(p)}(\textbf{q})\theta^{(n-p)}(\textbf{q'})\right]\ .

(81d)

Inserting (
81
) in the continuity equation one gets that for all
n\geq 1
,

n\delta^{(n)}(\textbf{k})-\theta^{(n)}(\textbf{k})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}}{2\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q'}}{2\pi}\delta^{(D)}(\textbf{k}-\textbf{q}-\textbf{q'})\alpha(\textbf{q},\textbf{q'})\\
\times\left[\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\theta^{(p)}(\textbf{q})\delta^{(n-p)}(\textbf{q'})\right]\ .

(82)

Now inserting the explicit dependence of
\delta^{(n)}
and
\theta^{(n)}
in terms of
\delta^{(1)}
and the kernels

F_{n}
and
G_{n}
, one gets the equality for the integrand of
\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n}

\left[nF_{n}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})-G_{n}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})\right]\delta^{(D)}(\textbf{k}-\textbf{q}|_{1}^{n})\\
=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}}{2\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q'}}{2\pi}\delta^{(D)}(\textbf{k}-\textbf{q}-\textbf{q'})\alpha(\textbf{q},\textbf{q'})\times\left[\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}G_{p}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{p})\delta^{(D)}(\textbf{q}-\textbf{q}|_{1}^{p})\right.\\
\left.F_{n-p}(\textbf{q}_{p+1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})\delta^{(D)}(\textbf{q}-\textbf{q}|_{p+1}^{n})\right]\ .

(83)

Killing the integral on
q
and
q’
, one gets the first recursion relation on the kernels

nF_{n}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})-G_{n}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})=\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\alpha\left(\textbf{q}|_{1}^{p},\textbf{q}|_{p+1}^{n}\right)G_{p}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{p})\\
\times F_{n-p}(\textbf{q}_{p+1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})\ .

(84a)

Similarly, by inserting (
81
) into the Euler equation, one gets the second recursion relation
on the kernels which reads,

\left(2n+1\right)G_{n}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})-3F_{n}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})=\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}2\beta\left(\textbf{q}|_{1}^{p},\textbf{q}|_{p+1}^{n}\right)G_{p}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{p})\\
\times G_{n-p}(\textbf{q}_{p+1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})\ .

(84b)

Finally, by combining (
84a
) and (
84b
) one obtains
the recursion relations for the gravitational kernels

\displaystyle F_{n}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\frac{G_{p}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{p})}{(2n+3)(n-1)}\left\{(2n+1)\alpha\left(\textbf{q}|_{1}^{p},\textbf{q}|_{p+1}^{n}\right)F_{n-p}(\textbf{q}_{p+1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})\right.

\displaystyle\phantom{=\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\frac{G_{p}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{p})}{(2n+3)(n-1)}\{}\left.+2\beta\left(\textbf{q}|_{1}^{p},\textbf{q}|_{p+1}^{n}\right)G_{n-p}(\textbf{q}_{p+1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})\right\}

(85)

\displaystyle G_{n}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\frac{G_{p}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{p})}{(2n+3)(n-1)}\left\{3\alpha\left(\textbf{q}|_{1}^{p},\textbf{q}|_{p+1}^{n}\right)F_{n-p}(\textbf{q}_{p+1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})\right.

\displaystyle\phantom{=\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\frac{G_{p}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{p})}{(2n+3)(n-1)}\{}\left.+2n\beta\left(\textbf{q}|_{1}^{p},\textbf{q}|_{p+1}^{n}\right)G_{n-p}(\textbf{q}_{p+1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n})\right\}

(86)

{exercisenb}

Two-loop power spectrum

Write down the the diagrams and integrals contributing to the two-loop
matter power spectrum in terms of the gravitational coupling kernels
F_{n}
.
Try to identify the diagrams with
the strongest UV-sensitivity.

SOLUTION:

Diagrams and integral at two-loop order

In perturbation theory, connected diagrams contributing to the power spectrum
P(\textbf{k})\equiv\left<\delta(\textbf{k})\delta(-\textbf{k})\right>
contains only two vertices
F_{n_{1}}
and
F_{n_{2}}

arising from the expansion of
\delta
functions using the ansatz (
78
). Using the topological identity
for connected planar graphs
L=I-V+1
where
L
is the number of loops,
I
the number of internal lines and
V
the number of vertices, leads to

n_{1}+n_{2}=6

(87)

for two-loop diagrams. Two-loop contributions to
P(\textbf{k})
corresponds to any contribution verifying (
87
).

Therefore
5
5

5

The time-dependence is omitted for clarity. Assuming the time-evolution factorizes at linear order leads to a simple power of linear growth factor
,

\displaystyle P_{\text{2-loop}}(\textbf{k})

\displaystyle=2\left<\delta^{(1)}(\textbf{k})\delta^{(5)}(-\textbf{k})\right>+2\left<\delta^{(2)}(\textbf{k})\delta^{(4)}(-\textbf{k})\right>+\left<\delta^{(3)}(\textbf{k})\delta^{(3)}(-\textbf{k})\right>\ ,

(88)

\displaystyle\equiv P_{15}(\textbf{k})+P_{24}(\textbf{k})+P_{33}(\textbf{k})\ ,

(89)

where
P_{n_{1}n_{2}}(\textbf{k})
corresponds to the sum of all diagrams with vertices
F_{n_{1}}
and
F_{n_{2}}
.
Assuming Gaussianity of
\delta^{(1)}(\textbf{k})
one can apply the Wick theorem leading to four distinct diagrams depicted in Fig.
14
.
Defining
F^{(s)}_{n}
as the symmetrized kernel
F_{n}
, associated integrals read as

\displaystyle P_{15}(\textbf{k})

\displaystyle=30\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\ F^{(s)}_{5}\left(\textbf{q}_{1},-\textbf{q}_{1},\textbf{q}_{2},-\textbf{q}_{2},\textbf{k}\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{=30\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}}\ \times P_{11}(\textbf{q}_{1})P_{11}(\textbf{q}_{2})P_{11}(\textbf{k})\ ,

(90a)

\displaystyle P_{24}(\textbf{k})

\displaystyle=24\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\ F^{(s)}_{4}\left(\textbf{q}_{1},\textbf{k}-\textbf{q}_{1},\textbf{q}_{2},-\textbf{q}_{2}\right)F^{(s)}_{2}\left(-\textbf{q}_{1},-(\textbf{k}-\textbf{q}_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{=30\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}}\ \times P_{11}(\textbf{q}_{1})P_{11}(\textbf{k}-\textbf{q}_{1})P_{11}(\textbf{q}_{2})\ ,

(90b)

\displaystyle P^{\text{R}}_{33}(\textbf{k})

\displaystyle=9\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\ F^{(s)}_{3}\left(\textbf{q}_{1},-\textbf{q}_{1},\textbf{k}\right)F^{(s)}_{3}\left(\textbf{q}_{2},-\textbf{q}_{2},-\textbf{k}\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{=30\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}}\ \times P_{11}(\textbf{q}_{1})P_{11}(\textbf{q}_{2})P_{11}(\textbf{k})\ ,

(90c)

\displaystyle P^{\text{I}}_{33}(\textbf{k})

\displaystyle=6\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\ F^{(s)}_{3}\left(\textbf{q}_{1},\textbf{q}_{2},\textbf{k}-\textbf{q}_{1}-\textbf{q}_{2}\right)F^{(s)}_{3}\left(-\textbf{q}_{1},-\textbf{q}_{2},-(\textbf{k}-\textbf{q}_{1}-\textbf{q}_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{=30\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\textbf{q}_{2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}}\ \times P_{11}(\textbf{q}_{1})P_{11}(\textbf{q}_{2})P_{11}(\textbf{k}-\textbf{q}_{1}-\textbf{q}_{2})\ ,

(90d)

where
P^{\text{R}}_{33}(\textbf{k})
and
P^{\text{I}}_{33}(\textbf{k})
correspond respectively to the
1
PR and
1
PI diagram with two
F_{3}
vertices.

Figure 14:
Two-loop diagrams contributing to the power spectrum.

Ultraviolet sensitivity

To analyze systematically the ultraviolet sensitivity of integrals in (
90
) one can in principle simply apply Weinberg’s asymptotic theorem
[
27
]
to the integrands.
However it quickly becomes cumbersome to analyze all possible subspaces of integration and the associated asymptotic coefficients of the gravitational coupling kernels
F^{(s)}_{n}
in these subspaces.
Instead one can derive an upper bound on the superficial degree of divergence
D_{\text{UV}}
for any diagram.

The linear power spectrum
P_{11}
scales as a power
n
of the momentum in the UV limit. Combined with a factor
3
for each loop integration leads to

D_{\text{UV}}\leq 3L-nI+\sum_{i}D(F^{(s)}_{n_{i}})

(91)

where the sum is on the set of vertices (inside a loop) and
D(F^{(s)}_{n_{i}})
is an upper bound on the asymptotic coefficients of
F^{(s)}_{n_{i}}
.
Since for
p\gg k

[
3
]

F^{(s)}_{n_{i}}(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{n_{i}-2},\textbf{p},-\textbf{p})\propto\frac{k^{2}}{p^{2}}\ ,

(92)

it is clear that
D(F^{(s)}_{n_{i}})\geq-2
. However, the asymptotic behavior of the kernel could be different when taking
more than two momenta in the UV limit. To easily obtain an upper bound on
D(F^{(s)}_{n_{i}})
one typically uses a decoupling argument from the UV physics
so that
D(F^{(s)}_{n_{i}})<0
(otherwise the mass fluctuation derived in perturbation theory would strongly depend on the UV initial conditions.) See for example
[
8
]
where it is claimed that
D(F^{(s)}_{n_{i}})<0
has been checked by explicit computation for
n_{i}\leq 6
(which is enough in our case.)
Then, because
F^{(s)}_{n_{i}}
is a rational function in the momenta
D(F^{(s)}_{n_{i}})
can only be an integer so that
D(F^{(s)}_{n_{i}})\leq-1
.
Thus,

D_{\text{UV}}\leq 3L-nI-V\ ,

(93)

where
V
is the number of vertices (inside a loop) of the diagram.
Eventually, using (
92
), (
93
) can be refined to

D_{\text{UV}}\leq 3L-nI-V-T\ ,

(94)

where
T
denotes the number of tadpoles of the diagram.

Applying (
94
) to two-loop diagrams leads to

\displaystyle D_{\text{UV}}(P_{15})

\displaystyle\leq 3+2n\ ,

(95a)

\displaystyle D_{\text{UV}}(P_{24})

\displaystyle\leq 3+3n\ ,

(95b)

\displaystyle D_{\text{UV}}(P^{\text{R}}_{33})

\displaystyle\leq 2+2n\ ,

(95c)

\displaystyle D_{\text{UV}}(P^{\text{I}}_{33})

\displaystyle\leq 4+3n\ .

(95d)

As seen in (
95
) the UV-sensitivity of a diagram depends on
n
.
From now on we assume the inequalities (
95
) to be saturated.
Regardless of
n

\displaystyle D_{\text{UV}}(P^{\text{R}}_{33})

\displaystyle\leq D_{\text{UV}}(P_{15})\ ,

(96a)

\displaystyle D_{\text{UV}}(P_{24})

\displaystyle\leq D_{\text{UV}}(P^{\text{I}}_{33})\ ,

(96b)

so that the most UV-sensitive two-loop diagram is either
P_{15}
or
P^{\text{I}}_{33}
.
One conclude that the most UV-sensitive diagram depends on the model.

If
n>-1
then the most UV-sensitive diagram is
P^{\text{I}}_{33}
else
it is
P_{15}
.

Note that to be complete, one should also check the superficial degree of divergence of any sub-diagram.
